Chapter 1
Introduction
An important goal of electronic structure theory is to ﬁnd bo und energy eigenstates of
atomic or molecular systems. In a non-relativistic setting (and in the framework of the
Born-Oppenheimer approximation [
1]), the ﬁrst step toward this goal can be reached by
solving the electronic time-independent Schrödinger equa tion:
H|Ψκ ⟩ =Eκ |Ψκ ⟩ .
Electronic states and the corresponding energy levels are i ndexed by κ. The real space
coordinate representation of the electronic Hamiltonian H reads (in ℏ =me =e2/(4πǫ0) = 1
atomic units)
H =
∑
i
(
−1
2 ∇2
i −
∑
A
ZA
|ri − RA|
)
+
∑
i<j
1
|ri − rj| ,
where ri and RA denote electronic and nuclear coordinates, and ZA are atomic numbers.
Knowing the electronic energy levels as functions of nuclea r coordinates (i.e. Eκ (R)), one
could take the next step and ﬁnd the bound states of nuclei; th is would completely determine
the bound states of the molecule on the Born-Oppenheimer lev el. In many cases, one
is interested only in the electronic ground state of the syst em; excited states will not be
discussed here at all.
Finding the exact solution of the Schrödinger equation is no t possible in the general N-
electron case, and one must resort to approximations: analy tical solutions only exist for
very special cases, and exact numerical solutions (obtaine d by e.g. Conﬁguration Interaction,
that is, by representing and diagonalizing H as a matrix in the space of N-electron basis
functions) usually come at extremely high computational co st.
The mean-ﬁeld approximation (as done in the Hartree-Fock, o r HF model) with perturbative
or Coupled Cluster (CC) corrections built on it often provid es very accurate approximations
to the exact ground state as long as correlation between elec trons is weak and the underlying
quasi-particle picture remains valid. However, these mode ls all tend to fail whenever
the strong correlation (entanglement) of electrons destro ys the quasi-particle picture: the
most common examples of such phenomena include molecules co nsiderably distorted from
equilibrium structure (containing dissociating covalent bonds) and open-shell systems. In
9
these cases, a better ﬁrst approximation is needed for the wa ve function than the one given
by mean-ﬁeld approximation. Such functions are generally c alled Multi-Reference (MR)
functions, and the generalized perturbative or CC methods b uilt on them are called MR
electronic structure methods. In the past decades, MR metho ds have given invaluable insight
into the structure of strongly correlated systems, yet, man y problems still remain unresolved.
This thesis is the summary of my doctoral research on the so-c alled internally contracted
MRCC (ic-MRCC) method. A new formulation of the theory is pre sented utilizing the
concept of dressing Hamiltonian interaction vertices by de nsity cumulants. These modiﬁed
vertices formally resemble their original "bare" counterp arts (the Fockian and the electron-
electron interaction vertices), hence they can be similarl y used as building blocks in the
Feynman diagrammatic construction of CC equations. The tra nsparent structure of the
formulae makes theoretical investigations (like investigation of dispersion interactions) rather
simple. The obtained ic-MRCC methods retain many nice featu res of HF-based CC, such as
formal simplicity, extensivity and tolerable computation al cost. At the same time, the MR
character of the underlying wave function is encoded in the m odiﬁed vertices through their
cumulant dressing, making them more suitable for strongly c orrelated scenarios. Analytical
and numerical investigation of this ic-MRCC formalism is th e central theme of the thesis;
performance of the method is examined with a so-called stron gly orthogonal geminal wave
function used as MR function.
Derivations in the thesis draw heavily on the MR generalizat ion of certain mathematical
techniques in many-body theory, such as cumulant expansion s, normal ordering of operators
and the Wick theorem. Despite their intuitive appeal and ele gance, these MR-generalized
tools (invented by Mukherjee and Kutzelnigg) have never ach ieved their due recognition
or seen widespread usage in quantum chemistry, most likely b ecause of the combinatorial
hardship associated with them. This is unfortunate, becaus e − in my opinion − these tools
could be the perfect candidates to bridge the gap between the conceptual simplicity of mean-
ﬁeld-based methods and the numerical eﬃciency of MR methods . A secondary goal of this
thesis is to present these MR tools in a uniﬁed and (mostly) se lf-contained manner, so that
it can be used as an introduction to the subject. This goal mig ht also serve as an excuse for
the somewhat long and detailed chapter on the theoretical ba ckground.
The thesis contains seven chapters. After this introductio n, Chapter
2 contains a review of
the aforementioned MR tools of many-body theory, geminal wa ve functions and CC. Chapter
3 describes the main theoretical developments concerning th e ic-MRCC with an emphasis
on diagrammatic techniques in derivations and the concept o f vertex dressing; a handful
of approximation schemes are also introduced here. Descrip tion of dispersion interaction
is the subject of Chapter 4, where emergence of the well-known London energy formula is
demonstrated for certain approximations of the MR theory. A more technical issue on the
linear dependence of elementary excitations used in MR calc ulations is addressed in Chapter
5. Finally, in Chapter 6, the numerical performance of these MRCC methods is tested o n
various strongly and weakly correlated molecular systems. The thesis is concluded with a
short discussion and outlook to possible future projects in Chapter 7.
10
Chapter 2
Theoretical background
In this chapter we try to give a detailed introduction to the b asic concepts of quantum
chemistry, which will be needed for the rest of the thesis.
In Sec.
2.1 we review the necessary mathematical tools of many-body the ory: the
second quantized formalism, reduced density matrices and c umulants, normal ordering of
operators and the generalized Wick theorem are all discusse d simultaneously in the single
determinantal and the multi-reference (MR) framework. In t his way one can see how well-
known concepts of single determinantal many-body theories can be naturally generalized in
a multi-determinantal setting.
This is followed by a brief introduction to two rather diﬀere nt approaches to electronic
structure calculations: a simple strong correlation model utilizing localized electron pairs
(geminals) in Sec.
2.2, and a weak correlation model built on a single Slater determ inant
(Coupled Cluster) in Sec. 2.3. We discuss the advantages and disadvantages of both method s.
A possible approach to combine weak and strong correlation m odels will be presented in the
next chapter.
2.1 Some mathematical tools of many-body theory
2.1.1 Second quantized operators
Derivations in the many-body theory can become quite cumber some if one has to deal
explicitly with the fermionic nature of wave functions. Thi s diﬃculty can be partially
lifted by switching to the so-called second quantized repre sentation. In the second quantized
formulation of quantum theory not only observables, but eve n wave functions are represented
in terms of operators: the proper antisymmetry with respect to interchanging particles is
encoded in the algebra of these operators. Here we shall revi ew the foundations of second
quantization without going too much into details; a compreh ensive treatment is given in
Refs. [
2–4], and a mathematically more rigorous one is given in Ref. [ 5]. Our other goal is
to make the reader comfortable with the − in this context somewhat uncommon − tensor
notation of second quantized operators. We shall strictly d istinguish between upper and
11
Some mathematical tools of many-body theory
lower indexed quantities as they are related to each other by hermitian conjugation. In the
context of second quantization, this notation was introduc ed by Harris et al. [
6] and further
popularized by Kutzelnigg [ 7–10].
Let us consider a basis {φp} spanned by M orthogonal one-electron functions (spin orbitals);
whileM is in principle inﬁnite, it is kept a large but ﬁnite number in all practical calculations.
In real space representation we have φp(x) = ⟨x|φp⟩ ; here x = (r,σ ) is used for brevity, with
r ∈ R3 and σ = α,β being the spatial and spin coordinates, respectively. Let F (M,N )
denote the Hilbert space of all antisymmetric N-electron states built with these orbitals. The
zero-particle sector contains only one state: F (M, 0) = {|0⟩} . This state is called the genuine
vacuum; ⟨ 0|0⟩ = 1 by deﬁnition. Slater determinants constructed from orbi tals φp1,...,φ pN
are denoted by |Φp1...p N ⟩ (or Φ p1...p N (x1,...,x N ) in real space); they form an orthonormal
basis in F (M,N ) (dim F (M,N ) =

2.1.2 Density matrices
The many-body wave function Ψ( x1,...,x N ) gives the complete probabilistic description of
our N-electron system, that is, the probability of ﬁnding N electrons at x1,...,x N . This
is much more information than what is actually needed in chem ical applications. Density
matrices provide a framework to extract the suﬃcient amount of information from the wave
function leading to a simpler and more compact description. Here we review the most
important basics of density matrices; the summary is mostly based on Refs. [
11, 12], other
references shall be marked explicitly.
Let us consider a normalized state |Ψ⟩ with corresponding density operator ρ = |Ψ⟩⟨ Ψ|. The
K-particle reduced density matrix ( K-RDM) is then deﬁned as
γp1...p K
q1...q K = NK Tr[ρap1...p K
q1...q K ] = NK⟨ap1...p K
q1...q K ⟩ , (2.1.12)
or as γK = NK⟨aK⟩ in a condensed notation. Here NK is a normalization factor, and
the notation ⟨...⟩ is reserved for expectation value taken with the reference s tate. A reduced
probabilistic description of the many-body system is encod ed in theK-RDM in the sense that
diagonal elements γp1...p K
p1...p K are proportional to the probability of ﬁnding electrons occ upying
spin orbitals φp1,...,φ pK . Similarly, for real space density matrices
γ(x1,...,x K;x′

of ﬁnding electrons at x1,...,x K; the relationship between the two representations of the
K-RDM is outlined in Appendix
A.
In order to avoid confusion, note that diﬀerent conventions are used for the normalization of
theK-RDM in the literature. Common choices include that of McWee ny (NK = 1), Löwdin
(NK = 1/K!) and Coleman ( NK = (N −K)!/N!). We shall use the McWeeny normalization
throughout this thesis; from now on NK = 1 is understood everywhere.
Reduced density matrices have a particularly simple form if |Ψ⟩ is just a single Slater

2.1.3 Density cumulants
Although density matrices provide a suﬃcient probabilisti c description of the N-electron
system, it is often practical to use quantities more closely related to correlation eﬀects:
among other appealing properties, this is the main motivati on to use density cumulants.
A formal deﬁnition of cumulants in many-body theory can be gi ven using a generating
function [
13, 17–19] (mirroring their original formulation in statistical phy sics [ 20]); we shall
come back to this in Sec. 2.1.5. However, in order to highlight their relation to correlati on,
we shall now introduce cumulants in a more heuristic way, thr ough an analogy with classical
probability theory.
Let Ω 1 and Ω 2 be two elements of an event algebra with associated probabil ites P (Ω1) and
P (Ω2), respectively. If these events were independent, their jo int probability would simply
be P (Ω1Ω2) = P (Ω1)P (Ω2). However, if the events are related to each other (i.e. they are
correlated) the joint probability cannot be simply express ed as a product, and a "correction
term" must be introduced:
P (Ω1Ω2) = P (Ω1)P (Ω2) + P(Ω1Ω2) .
The quantity P(Ω1Ω2) measures the deviation from the case of indepent events, an d therefore
accounts for the genuine correlation between Ω 1 and Ω 2. Note that P(Ω1Ω2) cannot be
strictly interpreted as a probability as it can also be negat ive (meaning that correlation
17
Some mathematical tools of many-body theory
makes Ω 1Ω2 less likely). For a single event Ω 1, it is natural to deﬁne P (Ω1) = P(Ω1).
Therefore, we have
P (Ω1Ω2) = P(Ω1)P(Ω2) + P(Ω1Ω2) .
For three events Ω 1, Ω 2 and Ω 3, the joint probability can be partitioned in a similar manne r:
P (Ω1Ω2Ω3) = P(Ω1)P(Ω2)P(Ω3) + P(Ω1Ω2)P(Ω3) + P(Ω2Ω3)P(Ω1)
+ P(Ω3Ω1)P(Ω2) + P(Ω1Ω2Ω3) .
The ﬁrst term refers to the case of three indepent events, the next three terms correspond
to correlation between two events (the third one being indep endent), and the last term
represents the fully correlated case. Following this recip e, one could partition any K-event
joint probability to a sum of products of K ′-event "connected probabilites" (for K ′ ≤K) by
considering all possible partitions of K.
In many-body theory the joint probability of ﬁnding K electrons at K points of either
orbital or real space is described by γK through Eqs. (
2.1.12) or ( 2.1.13). As a joint classical
probability having a purely product form describes indepen dent events, a density matrix
having a purely wedge product form (like the one in Eq. ( 2.1.12)) describes a system of
non-interacting (quasi-)particles. Pursuing this analog y, we ﬁnd that in a general K-RDM,
correction terms must be introduced to describe correlatio n eﬀects not covered by the 1-
RDM wedge product part. The antisymmetry of the K-RDM dictates the correction terms
to be also written in terms of wedge products. Then we have in c ondensed notation
γ1 =λ1 ,
γ2 =λ2 + 1

2λ2 ∧λ2 + 1
2λ2 ∧λ1 ∧λ1 + 1
24λ1 ∧λ1 ∧λ1 ∧λ1 , (2.1.26)
and in general

similarly to Eq. ( 2.1.16): it is given by wK1...K ν = n1!...nm!, the ni! being the number of

In the above sense, electron correlation is encoded in the co rrection terms to the γ1 wedge
product part of γK. Note, however, that there are other approaches to deﬁne ele ctron
correlation in the literature. According to the statistica l deﬁnition, electrons are correlated
if the probability of ﬁnding an electron at x1 is not completely independent of ﬁnding another
electron at x2. In this sense, almost every properly antisymmetric wave fu nction (even a
single determinant) describes correlation (the so-called Fermi correlation) due to ρ(x,x ) = 0,
i.e. the unability to detect same spin electrons in the same p oint of space. Another deﬁnition
given by Löwdin (to be brieﬂy mentioned in Sec.
2.1.5) refers to all eﬀects not covered by
the HF approximation as correlation eﬀects. The intrinsic d eﬁnition of correlation based on
cumulant expansion diﬀers from both approaches, as no refer ence is made to the underlying
wave function, and Fermi correlation is left out.
More details on the various deﬁnitions of electron correlat ion can be found in Ref. [
21].

2.1.4 Vanishing and non-vanishing of cumulants
Despite its nice features, cumulant expansion of the K-RDM has two drawbacks which
limit its general application. One is the rapidly increasin g combinatorial complexity of the
expansion: a simple excercise in combinatorics shows that t he expansion of the K-RDM
contains
RK =
K∑
ν =1
∑
K1,...,K ν ∈PK,ν
1
wK1...K ν
(
K!
K1!...Kν !
) 2
unique terms, which is already 131 for e.g. the 4-RDM. The oth er problem is the number
of elements in high rank cumulants: λK seemingly has M 2K entries, which quickly grows
intractable even in a smaller basis set. Although antisymme try and hermiticity properties
reduce the number of independent elements, it still grows to o rapidly with K and M.
Similar problems arise in the context of the Wick theorem (se e Sec. 2.1.6). As a way
out, expansions in terms of cumulants are truncated, usuall y by neglecting higher rank
cumulants. To understand the validity of such approximatio ns, it is essential to know under
what circumstances cumulant elements vanish, or at least be come small.
We begin with some exact results. Let us assume that the N electron wave function can be
written as the product of two independent NA- andNB-electron "groups" (withNA+NB =N),
that is, as |Ψ⟩ = |ΨA⟩ ⊗ | ΨB⟩ (we neglect antisymmetry to keep things simple). Orbitals
are grouped accordingly into two disjoint sets. The number o f nonzero intergroup density
matrix elements (containing orbitals from both groups) is n ow seriously reduced as creation-
annihilation operators can only act on the associated Fock s pace of either A or B. For
example:
γpA
sB = ⟨ ΨB| ⊗ ⟨ ΨA| (apAasB) |ΨA⟩ ⊗ | ΨB⟩ = ⟨ ΨA|apA|ΨA⟩⟨ ΨB|asB|ΨB⟩ = 0 .
The only kind of nonzero intergroup element of γ2 is γpArB
qAsB = γpA
qAγrB
sB . More generally, all
K-RDM elements factor into lower rank elements over the separ ate groups: this property
of density matrices is called multiplicative separability . We immediately ﬁnd that the
intergroupλ2 vanishes:
λpArB
qAsB =γpArB
qAsB −γpA
qAγrB
sB +γpA
sBγrB
qA = 0 .
From this it is quite obvious (but can also be proven formally [13, 19]) that intergroup
cumulants vanish for all K. This means that − unlike density matrices − cumulants are
additively separable over independent groups of the wave fu nction (so λK =λA
K ⊕λB
K).
An important result can be readily obtained as a special case : an occupied orbital (found in
all determinants of |Ψ⟩ ) can always be factored out to form a one-electron group as |Ψ⟩ =
|Ψ′⟩ ⊗ |φ⟩ . The fact that there is only one element in it, combined with t he antisymmetry
property of cumulants implies that cumulants vanish if at le ast one of their indices belongs
to an occupied orbital. Similarly, cumulants also vanish if one of their indices is virtual
(missing in all determinants of |Ψ⟩ ). This means that only active orbitals can contribute to
cumulants, and at most M 2K
a elements are to be considered for λK (Ma being the number
of active orbitals); this also sets an upper bound of Kmax =Ma for the rank of cumulants,
21
Some mathematical tools of many-body theory
as all higher rank cumulants vanish due to repeated indices a nd antisymmetry. Of course,
all cumulants are zero in the limit of |Ψ⟩ being purely a product of one-electron groups (i.e.
a single determinant).
Another kind of exact cancellation concerns odd-rank cumul ants only. As it was discussed
in Sec.
2.1.2, a state of the Fock space can be equally well described by spe cifying either
the occupied or the unoccupied states in determinants; in ot her words, γK and ηK have
equivalent roles. This relationship was further deepened i n Sec. 2.1.3 by noticing that
these quantities had almost identical cumulant expansions . It follows from comparing the
expansions of γK and ηK that odd rank cumulants vanish if γK =ηK for all K. This seems
very restrictive, but it makes sense in a special case. Let us consider a "half-ﬁlled" system with
Ma active orbitals and Na =Ma/2 electrons, whose wave function transforms as |Ψ⟩ → ±| Ψ⟩
under ﬂipping occupied and unoccupied states in each determ inant (e.g. |1001⟩ → | 0110⟩ ).
This transformation leaves density operator ρ = |Ψ⟩⟨ Ψ| invariant, and enforces a pairing of
determinants (for example, |1001⟩ and |0110⟩ can only appear as |1001⟩ ± | 0110⟩ ). Such a
system can describe e.g. the degenerate case of dissociated covalent bonds. It is easy to
show that in such a case γ
p1...p Na
q1...q Na =η
q1...q Na
p1...p Na =
(
η
p1...p Na
q1...q Na
) ∗
, hence γNa =ηNa if |Ψ⟩ is taken to
be real (as is done in most practical calculations). Compari son of Eqs. ( 2.1.17) and ( 2.1.23)
shows that γNa =ηNa automatically ensures the equality of all lower rank elemen ts. We can
then conclude that all odd-rank cumulants are zero if their i ndices belong to the active space
of completely dissociated covalent bonds [ 13].
Not much more can be said about the exact vanishing of cumulan ts without further specifying
the reference wave function. We shall now discuss under what circumstances cumulants can
be considered negligible.
Negligibility of cumulants is certainly justiﬁed in system s well described by a single Slater
determinant (e.g. closed-shell systems around equilibriu m geometry), where a wedge product
of K γ1-s is a good approximation of γK. In this case cumulants rapidly become less and
less important with increasing rank, as is supported by pert urbative arguments [
18]. But
the situation is more complicated in systems with a strong MR character. Dissociation of
multiple covalent bonds leads to a system of inﬁnitely separ ated high spin fragments which
are, however, still coupled to each other to form the correct spin state of the whole system.
Such an MR wave function can produce density matrices which a re vastly diﬀerent from
the wedge product, and one cannot expect the cumulants to be s mall in general. Odd-rank
cumulants are an exception: not only are they negligible aro und equilibrium geometry, but
they vanish in the dissociation limit, and therefore are ass umed to have little eﬀect in the
intermediate range, too. The importance of high rank cumula nts in the strongly correlated
regime was demonstrated analitically for simple model syst ems [ 23], and numerically for
realistic atomic, molecular systems [ 13, 24].
However, spin coupling does not occur if at least one of the tw o subsystems (denoted as A
and B) is a singlet. If they are separated by a distance large enoug h that exchange eﬀects
can be neglected, then the wave function is a product: |Ψ⟩ ≈ | ΨA⟩ ⊗ | ΨB⟩ , leading again to
product-like intersystem γK and vanishing intersystem λK. This property will be used in
Sec. 4.2, when investigating dispersion interactions between clos ed-shell systems.
Finally, a surprising feature of cumulants must be discusse d. When rank K exceeds the
22
Some mathematical tools of many-body theory
number of electrons in our system, K-particle density matrices naturally vanish by their
deﬁnition (cf. Eq. (
2.1.12)), but K-particle cumulants do not. Instead, they just become
factorized and completely expressed by lower rank density m atrices or cumulants. This
non-vanishing appears to contradict the usual physical or p robabilistic interpretation of
cumulants, asK-particle correlation does not seem to make any sense for K >N. Kutzelnigg
and Mukherjee coined the term Exclusion Principle Violatin g (EPV) cumulants [ 25] for these
strange cumulants following a loose analogy with perturbat ion theory [ 26, 27].
The meaning of EPV cumulants has remained a somewhat controv ersial topic in the
literature. Kong claimed that EPV cumulants were unphysica l due to their disconnected
nature, and proposed an ad-hoc modiﬁcation of Eq. ( 2.1.27) that ensured their cancellation
[28]; doing so, however, would lead to unwanted side eﬀects (see Sec. 3.2). Contrary to
this, Hanauer and Köhn argued that EPV cumulants are no less p hysical than ordinary
cumulants based on the symmetric role of occupied and unoccu pied states in Fock space [ 13].
Cumulants treat occupied and unoccupied states on an equal f ooting, as seen by e.g. λK
being the cumulant of both γK and ηK, or any other expectation value with K upper-lower
indices (up to a possible sign factor). Note that ηK has nonzero value for K ≤ M −N
only. From this point of view, existence of λK for K > N is no less meaningful than for
K >M −N, in which case ηK identically vanishes instead of γK (assuming an active space
of ﬁnite size). Cumulants simply provide a measure for corre lation between occupancy of
active orbitals, and this property is independent of the par ticle number.
All of the above general results will be demonstrated explic itly for cumulants of disjoint
two-electron group functions (so-called strongly orthogo nal geminals) in Sec. 2.2.2.
2.1.5 Normal ordering of operators
In various branches of physics, it is useful to decompose a ph ysical quantity X as a sum
of its expectation value (provided by some suitable averagi ng) and a (presumably small)
ﬂuctuation
X = ⟨X⟩ +δX , (2.1.34)
so that ⟨δX ⟩ = 0 and ⟨ (δX )2⟩ = ⟨X 2⟩ − ⟨ X⟩ 2. In the many-body problem, this average-
ﬂuctuation separation is implemented on the level of second quantized operators by their
so-called normal ordering.
In order to build some intuition about normal ordering, ﬁrst we deﬁne it for a system
described by a single Slater determinant [
26, 27, 29]. We shall refer to this determinant |Φ⟩
as the Fermi vacuum, and we shall distinguish between spin or bitals occupied (indexed by
i,j,k,l,... ) and unoccupied (indexed by a,b,c,d,... ) in it. Following a useful interpretation
originally rooted in quantum ﬁeld theory, we formally relat e the Fermi vacuum to the genuine
vacuum |0⟩ by calling occupied/unoccupied states hole/particle stat es. We deﬁne hole
creation-annihilation operators bi and bi accordingly: annihilation of an electron from an
occupied orbital is equivalent to the creation of a hole in th e Fermi vacuum and vice versa:
ai =bi andai =bi. Hole operators satisfy the same Grassmann algebra as parti cle operators,
and particle and hole operators anticommute with each other . Evidently, aa|Φ⟩ = |∅⟩ and
23
Some mathematical tools of many-body theory
bi|Φ⟩ = |∅⟩ , meaning that − in the particle-hole sense − the Fermi vacuum acts similarly to
the genuine one. Excitation aa
i |Φ⟩ =aabi|Φ⟩ can be formally interpreted as the polarization
of the vacuum by formation of a (virtual) particle-hole pair .
A product of creation-annihilation operators S is said to be normal ordered with respect to
the Fermi vacuum if − in the particle-hole sense − all annihilation operators are standing
right to the creation operators; normal ordering is the corr esponding rearrangement of
operators, denoted as : S:. All operators anticommute within normal ordering, and th e sign
of the ﬁnal product is determined by the parity of the permuta tion of operators compared
to the original order. A few simple examples include
:aiaa:=:biaa:= −aabi = −aaai , (2.1.35)
:aiakal:=:bibkbl:=bkblbi =akalai ,
:aaaiajabak:=:aabibjabbk:= −abaabibjbk = −abaaaiajak , (2.1.36)
:aaabajai:=:aaabbjbi:=aaabbjbi =aaabajai .
A normal ordered operator always satisﬁes
⟨ :S:⟩ = 0 , (2.1.37)
since : S: destroys the Fermi vacuum from either left (: S: |Φ⟩ = |∅⟩ ) or right ( ⟨ Φ| :S:= ⟨∅| ).
As long as our reference wave function is a single Slater dete rminant, the above prescription
completely determines normal ordering. But generally our s ystem is described by a linear
combination of determinants. This makes a complete deﬁniti on of normal ordering seemingly
hard, since diﬀerent orbitals are occupied and unoccupied i n the various determinants, and
thus no strict particle-hole classiﬁcation is possible. A w ay out of this problem can be
found by examining the relationship between "ordinary oper ators" and their normal ordered
counterparts ﬁrst in the single determinantal case: this le ads to the so-called Wick theorem.
It turns out that a new, equivalent deﬁnition of normal order ing can be found via the Wick
theorem that can be readily generalized for MR wave function s.
In what follows, we will use general second quantized operat ors αp which can be either
creation or annihilation operators; they are not distingui shed by upper or lower indices.
Indices p,q,r,s,... refer to general orbitals with unspeciﬁed particle-hole ch aracter. We
deﬁne the contraction between operators αp and αq as
αpαq =αpαq− :αpαq: .
It follows from the deﬁnition of normal ordering that the onl y two nonzero contractions are
aiak =δi

a .
Considering Eqs. ( 2.1.14) and ( 2.1.21), a contraction can be generally written as
αpαq = ⟨αpαq⟩ . (2.1.38)

Some mathematical tools of many-body theory
Note that the contraction can be nonzero only if αp and αq are not in normal order.
Now let us consider the product αp1αp2...αpK and its normal ordered counterpart
:αp1αp2...αpK :. The Wick theorem states that this product can be expressed as
αp1αp2...αpK =:αp1αp2...αpK : +

The proof of the Wick theorem is just roughly outlined; see Re f. [ 27] for a more detailed
justiﬁcation. As a base case, we immediately see that the the orem holds if αp1αp2...αpK is in
normal order, since then all contractions vanish. If one pai r of operators − sayαp1 and αp2
for the sake of simplicity − is not in normal order, and they do not anticommute with each
other, then their contraction is nonzero, and we can write
αp2αp1αp3...αpK = {αp2,αp1}αp3...αpK −αp1αp2αp3...αpK
= {αp2,αp1} :αp3...αpK : − :αp1αp2αp3...αpK :
=:αp2αp1αp3...αpK : + :αp2αp1αp3...αpK : .
All other contractions are zero due to the other operators be ing in normal order, hence the
theorem holds. One could continue this way to show that all op erator pairs out of the normal
order give rise to contractions, and thus prove the Wick theo rem.
Based on the ﬁndings of Sec. 2.1.3 (cf. Eqs. ( 2.1.28), ( 2.1.32), and ( 2.1.33), now without
cumulants) and Eq. ( 2.1.38), multiple contractions in the Wick expansion can always be
grouped into a single expectation value. The example of Eq. ( 2.1.39) then takes the form
αpαqαrαs = :αpαqαrαs:
+ ⟨αpαq⟩ :αrαs: −⟨αpαr⟩ :αqαs: +⟨αpαs⟩ :αqαr:
+ ⟨αqαr⟩ :αpαs: −⟨αqαs⟩ :αpαr: +⟨αrαs⟩ :αpαq:
+ ⟨αpαqαrαs⟩ .
The signs are simply given by ( −1)π , π being the parity of index permutation compared to
the original order (i.e. the number of transpositions requi red to reorder the operators).

It is important to note that the Wick theorem (like Eq. ( 2.1.42) for excitation operators) is
not simply a consequence of the original particle-hole-bas ed normal ordering deﬁnition, but is
completely equivalent to it. We could have used the Wick "the orem" to deﬁne normal ordered
operators and we could have derived the rearrangement relat ions (such as Eqs. ( 2.1.35) and
(2.1.36)) from it. However, the Wick theorem has the advantage that i t does not make
any explicit reference to particles, holes, or the structur e of the underlying wave function:
any MR wave function could be used as long as the arising expec tation values ⟨αp1...αpK′ ⟩
are properly calculated. Therefore, we can actually take th ese relations as the deﬁnition of
normal ordered operators with respect to an arbitrary MR wav e function, and thus obtain a
general tool with all the desirable properties of ordinary n ormal ordering retained (such as
Eq. ( 2.1.37)). In the literature this approach − originating from Kutzelnigg and Mukherjee
− is known as the Multi-Reference Generalized Normal Orderin g (MRGNO) [ 9, 30–32].
The MRGNO can be easily formulated in terms of contractions i nstead of expectation values
by means of the cumulant expansion. The only diﬀerence is tha t, in addition to the one-
particle contraction of Eq. ( 2.1.38), we must have general K-particle contractions (that is,
cumulants) as well:
αp1αp2...αp2K = ⟨αp1αp2...αp2K ⟩c . (2.1.43)
Then the MRGNO expansion of an operator is obtained by consid ering all possible K-particle
contractions of its normal ordered counterpart. The MRGNO v ersion of Eq. ( 2.1.39) is for
example appended with αpαqαrαs = ⟨αpαqαrαs⟩c, while for a3 we would ﬁnd

A word of caution: while normal ordering is perfectly well de ﬁned as a prescription for
arranging elements in a product, it must not be thought of or a pplied as an operator acting
on some vector space. Operations like "normal ordering both sides of an equation" can
quickly lead to contradictions and should be avoided. For ex ample, by normal ordering both
sides of {aa,ab} = δb
a and using the fact that operators within normal order antico mmute,
one could "prove" 0 = δb
a.
There are alternative or further generalized extensions of normal ordering for MR wave
functions, such as the quasi-normal ordering of Kong et al. [ 33], or the generalized
antisymmetric ordered product of Sinha et al. [ 34]. When applied to a single determinant,
the traditional normal ordering is recovered by these diﬀer ent orderings, but they are not
equivalent in general. For example, the quasi-normal order ing does not satisfy Eq. ( 2.1.37)
for an MR wave function. We shall not discuss these alternati ve orderings here in any more
detail.

2.1.6 The generalized Wick theorem
In Sec.
2.1.5 we saw how normal ordering of operators can be used to partiti on physical
quantities to a mean and a ﬂuctuation part. In order to make MR GNO a really useful
tool when dealing with the many-body problem, the multiplic ation rules of normal ordered
operators must be worked out. This set of rules is collective ly known as the generalized Wick
theorem: it states that a product of normal ordered operator s :S1: and : S2: is given by a
Wick-like expansion generated by all K-body contractions of the "merged" operator : S1S2:,
written symbolically as
:S1::S2:=:S1S2: + :S1S2: .
Only contractions connecting the two operators are needed, as self-contractions like S1
vanish. Similarly to normal ordering, the generalized Wick theorem with respect to a
single Slater determinant (the Fermi vacuum) is well known a nd widely used [ 26, 27], while
its MR generalization (introduced by Mukherjee and Kutzeln igg [ 30–32]) is somewhat less
recognized. The theorem can be proven by induction [ 19, 33, 34]. However, we will only
demonstrate its validity for simple cases through explicit calculation.
The ﬁrst few examples are trivial consequences of normal ord ering (cf. Eqs. ( 2.1.40) and
(2.1.5)):

As a general rule, we can say that product of two MRGNO operato rs :S1: and :S2: is evaluated
by considering all possible contractions between them, eac h contraction producing a K-
body cumulant (with γ1 = λ1 understood). The one exception under this rule is the pair-
contraction between an annihilation operator of : S1: and a creation operator of : S2:, which
yields η1.
The sign of the various terms is again given by ( −1)π , π being the parity of permutation.
When dealing with products of excitation operators, there i s a simpler way to determine
the sign, by counting the parity of upper and lower indices ( πU and πL) independently; the
reason for this is similar to the one discussed for normal ord ering. The only diﬀerence is
that this method would count the index order of ηp
q = ⟨aqap⟩ incorrectly as pq and not as
qp. Because of this, an extra factor of ( −1)nη appears for the nη η1-s in the term: the sign
is then given by ( −1)π U +π L−nη . Signs of terms can be easily evaluated this way in e.g. Eqs.
(
2.1.52) and ( 2.1.53).
The above rules for evaluating products of normal ordered op erators with respect to a general
MR wave function are collectively known as the Multi-Refere nce Generalized Wick Theorem
(MRGWT). If the reference function is a single determinant, all cumulants vanish leaving
only γ1-s and η1-s, and the more common Wick theorem is recovered. Since mult iplication
of operators is associative and distributive with addition , product of an arbitrary number of
normal ordered operators can be evaluated with the successi ve application of the MRGWT.
If one is interested in the expectation value of a product, it is suﬃcient to evaluate only the

by virtue of Eq. ( 2.1.37). Closing this section, we present the expression for ⟨ :a2::a2:⟩ , as we

2.1.7 Spin-free formalism
Up to this point, our entire discussion has been based on spin orbitals, without explicitly
referencing spin degrees of freedom. This is quite useful wh en developing theories as all
quantities can be conveniently expressed in terms of antisy mmetric tensors. But in the end,
these theories must be implemented in a way that ensures the p roper spin symmetry of the
system: that is, our models are to be adapted to the desired sp in state. In this subsection
− contrary to earlier ones − latin indices correspond to spatial orbitals and spin indic es are
written out explicitly; a spin orbital index is then a compos ite index pσ made up of spatial
partp = 1,...,M/ 2 and spin part σ =α,β . Throughout the thesis we shall always point out
if spatial orbitals are used in a section or subsection. When not stated otherwise explicitly,
latin indices will keep referring to spin orbitals.
The spin state of N-electron systems is characterized by the eigenvalues of th e third
component and the squared magnitude of the spin operator ( ˆSz and ˆS2, respectively).
The reason for this is that ˆSz and ˆS2 can be simultaneously measured, meaning that
[ ˆSz, ˆS2] = 0. As a consequence, a common set of eigenvectors {|S,MS⟩} (forS = 0, 1/2, 1,... ,
and MS = −S, −S + 1,...,S ) can be found:
ˆSz|S,MS⟩ =MS|S,MS⟩ , ˆS2|S,MS⟩ =S(S + 1)|S,MS⟩ .
See e.g. Ref. [
2] for the second quantized form of spin operators. In this the sis we shall be
concerned with singlet states (i.e. the case of S = 0, MS = 0).
In a non-relativistic theory the Hamiltonian does not conta in any spin-dependent
interactions. This means that the matrix elements of Eqs. ( 2.1.7) and ( 2.1.8) are diagonal
in their spin indices: hqσ 1

Many-body theories written purely in terms of spin-free qua ntities have many desirable
properties. The most important one is that a spin-free corre ction method built on some
reference wave function |ΦS,M S ⟩ will not contaminate the original spin: as a consequence of
all operators commuting with ˆSz and ˆS2, the corrected wave function will remain the very
same spin eigenstate. Also, dealing with M/2 spatial orbitals instead of M spin orbitals in
our equations may lead to a great reduction in computational eﬀorts (roughly a factor of 2 K

for K nested summations).
The only question is whether one can easily formulate a theor y in terms of spin-free

or cumulants) appearing in it. This spin-summation is alway s straightforward for a single
tensor, as seen in Eqs. (

this problem when trying to perform the spin-summation of te rms in cumulant expansions

Geminal wave functions
2.2 Geminal wave functions
A wave function consisting of a single Slater determinant ca nnot properly describe strong
correlation eﬀects. It is well known [
1] that a chemical bond treated on single determinantal
level is described by a mixture of ionic and covalent contrib utions, no matter how stretched
the bond is; ionic terms (with excess electrons localized on one of the atomic sites) do not
vanish even in the dissociation limit, leading to a qualitat ively wrong wave function and
an anomalously high ground state energy. An MR wave function is needed to get at least
qualitatively good results along the complete dissociatio n process.
If ﬁnding the best one-electron functions (as is done in the H F approximation) is not good
enough for our goals, then − as the logical next step − we might try to determine the best
two-electron functions or electron pairs. These two-elect ron functions are called geminals.
In geminal models the whole N-electron wave function (N assumed to be even) is built from
such two-electron blocks. In many cases this is in very good a ccordance with the chemist way
of thinking; after all, most of our chemical intuition is gai ned from models in which molecules
are formed by localized electron pairs as bonds. Such consid erations − in the form of Valence
Bond theory − go back to the early days of quantum chemistry (see Ref. [ 39] for historical
perspectives and applications).
We will be concerned with so-called strongly orthogonal and singlet coupled geminal wave
functions only. In the following section, basic properties of such wave functions with their
corresponding density matrices and cumulants will be discu ssed. More details on this subject
with an extensive list of references can be found in Refs. [
40–43].
Since spin states of geminals must be taken care of, spatial a nd spin indices are written
separately throughout this whole section; latin indices de note spatial orbitals accordingly.
2.2.1 General considerations
Let us consider sets of spin orbitals {φpσ }P ; these could in principle be any kind of subsets of
M spin orbitals. Furthermore, let the set contaning the spati al indices appearing in {φpσ }P
be denoted as GP .
The general form of a two-electron function built from orbit als of {φpσ }P reads (cf. Eq.
(
2.1.1))
|ψP ⟩ = 1

geminals in either real or orbital space means

the statement is also true: strong orthogonality implies th e mutually disjoint distribution
of orbitals among geminals. The equivalence of these two con ditions is known as the Arai
theorem [ 44].
Strongly orthogonal geminals are much easier to handle and u se as building blocks. Acting on
the vacuum with the product of N/2 (diﬀerent) geminal creation operators yields a simple
N-electron wave function Ansatz, the Antisymmetrized Produ ct of Strongly Orthogonal

The antisymmetrizer (according to its deﬁnition in Appendi x

are independent two-electron groups in the same sense as was used in Sec. 2.1.4.
Two electrons can be coupled to form either a singlet or a trip let state, and coeﬃcients of
geminal wave functions should be chosen accordingly. We shall only deal with the singlet case,
that is, we require MS = 0 and S = 0 for all geminals. The former can be easily achieved by
restricting the coeﬃcients to have only one α and oneβ spin index. The latter can be ensured
by choosing the geminal coeﬃcient matrix to be symmetric wit h respect to interchanging
spatial indices. Then we have (in accordance with Eq. ( C.0.14))cP
p1σ 1p2σ 2 =cP

natural orbitals.
APSG belongs to the family of Multiconﬁgurational Self-Con sistent Field (MCSCF)
methods: orbitals and geminal coeﬃcients must be simultane ously optimized in order to
arrive at the lowest APSG energy (cf. Eq. (
2.1.19); see Sec. 2.2.2 for the density
matrices). At the end of the complicated variational optimi zation procedure one obtains
unique geminals localized to certain areas within the molec ule (between atoms or around a
single atom), which represent bonding and lone electron pai rs according to the usual chemical
interpretation.
Loosely speaking, APSG separates electron correlation int o an intrageminal and an
intergeminal part. While intrageminal correlation is prop erly taken into account
(corresponding to the fact that a variational problem is sol ved for each electron pair),
intergeminal correlation is not included. This is due to the Ansatz being a product of
strongly orthogonal geminals, meaning that interaction of electron pairs is still described
only on a mean ﬁeld level.
The values of geminal coeﬃcients can be guessed in the limiti ng case of weak correlation
(around equilibrium geometry of a bonding geminal or in a lon e pair geminal) and strong
correlation (in a dissociating bonding geminal). In the for mer case a mean ﬁeld description of
the electron pair is expected to be suﬃcient, the geminal is d ominated by a single two-electron
determinant with a coeﬃcient close to unity, all other deter minants having negligible weight.
In the latter case two determinants (say the p-th and r-th) have equal weight in the geminal
and all the others vanish. The two corresponding coeﬃcients are then cP
p = −cP
r = 1
√
2;
the sign diﬀerence can be guessed from the requirement that t he wave function describes a
purely covalent system with one electron localized on each o f the two atomic sites [ 1,46] (see
also Appendix B).
The number of spatial orbitals assigned to geminals is an adj ustable parameter of the theory.
35
Geminal wave functions
Obviously, a geminal containing only one orbital (i.e. ψP =apαpβ ) accounts for treating the
corresponding electron pair on a spin restricted mean ﬁeld l evel. The simplest improvement
over mean ﬁeld treatment requires two orbitals:
ψP =cP
papαpβ +cP
rarαrβ .
The special case of APSG wave function built with geminals co ntaining at most two orbitals
(i.e. |GP | ≤ 2) is called Generalized Valence Bond (GVB) [
47–49]. We shall use GVB in all
practical calculations.
APSG has numerous advantages. It represents a conceptually and computationally simple
improvement over mean ﬁeld techniques and can accurately de scribe the dissociation of a
single covalent bond. The APSG energy always represents an u pper bound for the exact
ground state energy, as orbitals and coeﬃcients are determi ned variationally. Furthermore,
it gives the exact variational solution for two-electron sy stems, provided that all orbitals are
assigned to the geminal (i.e. |G| = M/2). The APSG energy is also size consistent and
extensive over singlet subsystems.
However, there are also disadvantages. While APSG recovers a large part of static electron
correlation, it fails to incorporate much of dynamic correl ation, which would be needed
for accurate energies and molecular properties. This weakn ess of APSG is manifested e.g.
in its inability to properly describe intermolecular (e.g. dispersion) interactions, as these
would require a more reﬁned treatment of interbond interact ions than mean ﬁeld. Several
correction methods have been proposed to add dynamic correl ation to APSG, including both
perturbative [
50, 51] and non-perturbative [ 42, 52–59] approaches.
Also, APSG relies heavily on the localized nature of chemica l bonds in molecules (i.e. the
existence of a single dominant Lewis structure), and theref ore cannot be properly applied to
e.g. delocalized π-electron systems.
There is a more subtle problem too, concerning the APSG wave f unction. While APSG yields
the correct wave function for single bond dissociation (des cribing two inﬁnitely separated, but
spin coupled doublet subsystems), the same is not true for mu ltiple bond dissociations [ 60,61].
A simple example is provided by the symmetric dissociation o f the H 2O molecule: we know
from the Hund rule that in the inﬁnite separation limit the sy stem should consist of a
triplet oxygen atom and two triplet coupled hydrogen atoms, all coupled together to form
a singlet state. In the APSG description, however, the syste m consists of two singlet O-H
electron pairs, no matter how stretched the bonds are: essen tially, spin coupling between
electrons of diﬀerent bonds is missing. One thus generally c oncludes that APSG yields a
qualitatively wrong wave function for multiple bond dissoc iations. This error may seriously
eﬀect correction methods using APSG as starting point, and m ay generate unphysical local
maxima or even singularities on potential energy surfaces [ 53, 54].

Cancellation of intergeminal contributions to cumulants i s the manifestation of the general
result in Sec. 2.1.4. This important property leads to the vanishing of suﬃcient ly high
rank cumulants. If |GP | spatial orbitals (that is, 2 |GP | spin orbitals) are assigned to the
P -th geminal, than any possible cumulant of rank K > 2|GP | would contain at least one
repeated upper/lower index and vanish by virtue of antisymm etry. Therefore, the highest
cumulant rank for APSG is Kmax = 2 maxP (|GP |), and Kmax = 4 for GVB [ 18]. Note that
this vanishing is independent of the particle number: while λ3 orλ4 in GVB can be nonzero
for a two-electron system, λK with K >4 is always zero.

As an illustration of cumulant magnitudes in a single GVB gem inal, the squared norm of
ΛK is plotted in Fig. 2.1 for K = 2, 3, 4 as a function of the smaller of the two occupation
numbers,n<. The squared norm is deﬁned as ||ΛK||2 = Tr[ΛKΛK]/K!. Around equilibrium
bond distance the geminal is dominated by a single determina nt, n< ≈ 0 and cumulants
are negligible, as expected. On the other hand, both Λ 2 and Λ 4 become quite large in the
dissociating limit (where n< ≈ 1/2). The Λ 3 vanishes in both limiting cases, having only a
tiny maximum around n< ≈ 0.2.

Orbitals assigned to geminals are localized around atoms or bonds, and so are cumulants.
For spatially well separated electron pairs, Λ P (r1, r2; r′
1, r′
2) takes a non-negligible value only
if points r1, r2, r′
1, r′
2 are all in the vicinity of the P -th pair. The above formulae again signal
the fact that while intrabond correlation is taken care of by strongly orthogonal geminals,
interbond correlation is not: ﬁnding an electron around a bo nd and ﬁnding a second electron
around a diﬀerent bond are essentially uncorrelated events .
As demonstrated in Appendix
B, cumulants are essential in realizing the so-called left-r ight
correlation in bonds, that is, the proper localization of el ectrons in the dissociating limit.
Besides the advantages, cumulants naturally inherit the di sadvantages of the APSG wave
function, mostly the erroneous spin coupling in multiple bo nd dissociations. Looking at the
example of symmetric H 2O dissociation, APSG cumulants do describe the correct loca lization
of electrons on the oxygen atom, but they lack the coupling te rms necessary for a triplet
state. This weakness will have some troubling eﬀects on APSG -based correction methods
employing cumulants.
2.3 The Coupled Cluster model
In general, the exact wave function of the N-electron system is a combination of multiple
determinants. However, in many important cases, the wave fu nction is dominated by
dynamic electron correlation, i.e. it is well approximated by a single determinant (built
from e.g. HF orbitals), while other determinants give only m inor contributions; closed-shell
molecules around their equilibrium structure are the main e xamples. In such cases these small
corrections can be easily introduced by means of many-body p erturbation theory (MBPT).
Assuming the convergence of the MBPT series, one expects mor e and more accurate results
by calculating successively higher order contributions to the energy and the wave function.
Unfortunately, with increasing order, the equations quick ly become intractable and the
computational cost increases dramatically.
The Coupled Cluster (CC) model provides a very eﬃcient alter native approach to incorporate
dynamic correlation. Introduced originally in the context of the nuclear many-body problem
by Coester and Kümmel [
62], CC was largely reformulated and improved on by Čížek [ 63,64].
Since then, following the works of many (e.g. Refs. [ 65, 66] just to name a few), CC has
become perhaps the most successful tool in ab initio quantum chemistry. Instead of an exact
calculation of the ﬁrst few orders of MBPT, CC aims to take app roximately all orders of
MBPT into account. This approximate resummation can be seen most easily by formulating
the theory in terms of Feynman diagrams, as will be outlined b elow.
The following summary is mostly based on Refs. [ 26, 27, 67, 68]; all other references will be
shown explicitly.
40
The Coupled Cluster model
2.3.1 General considerations
The exact wave function |Ψ⟩ can be expanded in the basis of Slater determinants, as seen
in Eq. (

assumed, it is useful to express all other determinants of th e expansion relative to |Φ⟩ with
the help of excitation operators:
|Ψ⟩ = |Φ⟩ +

Indicesi,j,k,l,... anda,b,c,d,... denote the occupied and unoccupied (virtual) spin orbitals
(or equivalently, hole and particle states) of |Φ⟩ , respectively. Eq. (
2.3.1) is interpreted as
taking all possible polarizations of the Fermi vacuum into a ccount by formation of particle-
hole pairs (i.e. aa1...a K

of |Φ⟩ reﬂects the so-called intermediate normalization of |Ψ⟩ (fulﬁlling ⟨ Φ|Ψ⟩ = 1). In
principle, one could determine all coeﬃcients variational ly and thus obtain exact solutions
to the Schrödinger equation, as done in CI. In practice, howe ver, this is possible only for
very small systems. Truncating the expansion after a ﬁnite e xcitation rank (e.g. K = 2)
signﬁcantly decreases the computational cost, but tends to yield poor results violating size
consistency and extensivity.

and so on. Following the analogy with the cumulant expansion of density matrices,
amplitudes can be thought of as "irreducible" components of CI coeﬃcients. Due to the
exponential form of Eq. (
2.3.2), even a truncated T -expansion preserves size extensivity.
The next question is how to ﬁnd amplitudes tK. Substituting |Ψ⟩ =:exp(T ): |Φ⟩ into the
Schrödinger equation gives
H :exp(T ): |Φ⟩ = ∆E :exp(T ): |Φ⟩ .
The normal product Hamiltonian was deﬁned in Eq. ( 2.1.44), and the energy correction
is ∆E = Eexact − ⟨H⟩ . Projecting the equation by ⟨ Φ| and exploiting the intermediate
normalization gives a simple energy formula:
∆E = ⟨H :exp(T ):⟩ . (2.3.3)
A similar projection by excited determinants {⟨ Φ| :ai1...i K
a1...a K :} (K = 1, 2,...,N ) leads to a set
of independent equations for each unknown amplitude:
⟨ :ai1...i K
a1...a K : H :exp(T ):⟩ = ∆E⟨ :ai1...i K
a1...a K ::exp(T ):⟩ .
One can show that this amplitude equation can be simpliﬁed to
⟨ :ai1...i K
a1...a K : H :exp(T ):⟩C = 0 , (2.3.4)
where capital C in the subscript denotes the connected expectation value of the operator
product: each operator must be connected to the rest of the pr oduct by a Wick-contraction
of at least one pair of indices. The proof of this result is pos tponed until Sec. 3.1, where we
will verify it in a more general MR setting.
Note that we could truncate the T -expansion anywhere, and we would still have the right
amount of equations after projecting with the appropriate e xcited determinants. The most
common choices for truncated expansions correspond to keep ing only double excitations
(CCD) or single and double excitations (CCSD). In this work, we shall be mostly concerned
with these two approaches..
Eqs. (
2.3.3) and ( 2.3.4) are the standard equations of CC. To proceed further, the
expectation values of their operator products must be evalu ated using the GWT. In general,
this would be quite tedious; however, the work can be conside rably reduced with the
diagrammatic methods introduced in the next section.

and (2.1.21)). For this reason, the exponential operator is always trun cated after the (K +2)-
th order (K being the rank of the excitation operator on the left, 2 being the highest rank

This purely algebraic approach becomes very cumbersome for the general amplitude
equations. To make things easier, an intuitive graphical no tation is used instead, originally
introduced by Feynman in the context of quantum ﬁeld theory ( and later redesigned by
Goldstone, Hugenholtz and others for MBPT [ 29, 69]). Let us represent the parameters of
the Hamiltonian with the following dashed horizontal lines :
f ⇔

/A2

The number of dots indicates the rank of the quantity (i.e. th e number of upper-lower index
pairs attributed to it). The × symbol of the Fockian serves as a reminder that, although a
one-particle quantity,f still carries an implicit two-particle contribution due to Eq. ( 2.1.46).
Analogously, CC amplitudes shall be represented by continu ous horizontal lines:

The above horizontal lines shall be referred to as vertices. Hole or particle indices
(originating from γ- or η-type pair contractions) are represented by oriented lines entering
and leaving the dots of vertices. By deﬁnition, down- and upg oing lines correspond to hole
and particle indices, while incoming and outgoing lines den ote upper and lower indices,
respectively. Examples of Hamiltonian matrix elements inc lude

In the algebraic approach, all possible contractions must b e considered when applying the
GWT. Equivalently, in the diagrammatic approach, all possi ble connectivity patterns must
be drawn: for example, three unique diagrams for the three un ique terms of Eq. ( 2.3.5).
Since all CC equations are linear in H, each diagram must contain only one Hamiltonian
vertex; this can be connected to an arbitrary number of ampli tude vertices. Connecting
a quantity to itself is forbidden: this reﬂects the fact that self-contractions are omitted
in a normal ordered formalism. Similarly to the energy formu la, one can also represent
all unique terms of the amplitude equations with connected d iagrams, the only diﬀerence
being the appearance of 2 K free (uncontracted) fermion lines belonging to the indices of the
projecting determinants in Eq. ( 2.3.4). This highlights the usefulness of the diagrammatic
approach: in this way, instead of calculating a large number of equivalent terms in GWT and
then manually simplifying the results, one can immediately generate the unique contributions
only.
The only question left is how to obtain the ﬁnal form of expres sions from these drawings.
The so-called diagram rules of CC provide a one-to-one map to readily translate diagrams
to algebraic expressions. The proof of these interpretatio n rules will be brieﬂy discussed in
Sec.
3.2.1; here we shall only quote them:
• A1: Write the product of algebraic expressions correspond ing to interaction and
amplitude vertices.
• A2: Assign a hole or particle index to each down- or upgoing l ine.
• A3: Sum over the indices of each internal line.
• A4: Assign a phase factor ( −1)H−L, withH andL standing for the number of hole lines
and closed fermion loops (including quasi-loops connectin g external lines), respectively.
• A5: Assign a symmetry factor of 1 /2 for each equivalent pair of internal lines (same
particle-hole character, connecting the same two quantiti es) and 1/2 for each equivalent
pair of amplitudes.
• A6: Antisymmetrize all external upper/lower indices that are not manifestly
antisymmetric.
These rules are suﬃcient to obtain Eq. (
2.3.5) from Eq. ( 2.3.6). None of the diagrams
produce a sign, since they all have the same number of hole lin es and fermion loops. The
second diagram contains a factor of (1 /2)2 due to a pair of equivalent hole lines and a
pair of equivalent particle lines. In the third diagram ther e are no equivalent lines but a
pair of equivalent t1 amplitudes, hence the factor of 1 /2. Note that the vertices represent

diagrams. For example, the second diagram could also be draw n as

The Coupled Cluster model
The CCSD approximation already recovers all ﬁrst-order wav e function diagrams and all
second- and third-order energy diagrams shown above. The mi ssing three-body and higher
rank excitations start contributing to the wave function in second order and to the energy
in fourth order. This is the reason why CCSD performs so well i n all cases where good
convergence of MBPT can be expected. When working with HF orb itals (meaning fi
a = 0),
contributions of one-body excitations to the ﬁrst-order wa ve function and to the second- and
third-order energy vanish; for this reason, HF-CCD and HF-C CSD results tend to be rather
similar.
Many diﬀerent connectivity patterns ("topologies") can be identiﬁed in the above diagrams;
some of the most important ones are referred to as ring-, ladd er- and mosaic-type diagrams.
Ring diagrams describe purely hole-particle excitations a t each intermediate state (i.e. at
each ﬁctitious horizontal line between Hamiltonian vertices): the third diagram of Eq. (
2.3.9)
(or equivalently the one in Eq. ( 2.3.10)) is a ring diagram linear in t. The ﬁrst and
second diagrams of Eq. ( 2.3.9) are called hole-hole and particle-particle ladder diagra ms,
as their two-electron vertex describes hh → hh and pp → pp scattering, respectively. The
inhomogenous term of Eq. ( 2.3.8) (the "bare" hole-particle vertex) can be classiﬁed as both
ring- and ladder-type. The last skeleton in the second line o f Eq. ( 2.3.8) stands for the
non-linear ring and ladder diagrams.
Some well-known approximations of CCD are based on keeping o nly certain classes of
diagrams, such as ring-CCD, or ladder-CCD [
70]. These approximations often have an
interesting relationship with other electronic structure models; equivalence of (a certain
type of) ring-CCD and the Random Phase Approximation (RPA) h as been noticed by
many [63, 71, 72].
The so-called mosaic diagrams are represented by the ﬁrst sk eleton of the third line in Eq.
(2.3.8). Note that these diagrams can be united with the hole-hole a nd particle-particle
Fockian diagrams (represented by the second skeleton of the ﬁrst line) after introducing
/A1

The interpretation of this is that mosaic diagrams provide a screening eﬀect to the "bare"
Fockian vertices.
We shall revisit this concept and the role of the various diag ram classes (especially ring
diagrams and RPA) later in the framework of MRCC.
2.3.3 Advantages and disadvantages of Coupled Cluster
The CC model has many appealing features even from a formal po int of view. Its
exponential Ansatz combined with the manifestly connected nature of CC equations ensures
size consistency and extensivity of the results as long as al l Hamiltonian vertices between
the non-interacting subsystems vanish. This property hold s, no matter how the expansion
of T is truncated.
A truncated CC approach with T =T1 +... +TK gives exact results for systems containing
49
The Coupled Cluster model
N ≤ K electrons: CCSD is therefore exact for two-electron system s, such as the hydrogen
molecule. This also explains why CCSD can often describe wel l the dissociation of single
covalent bonds, while ﬁnite-order MBPT breaks down.
The CC energy is invariant under unitary mixing of orbitals i n the occupied and virtual
subspaces, giving e.g. the same value for canonical and loca lized HF orbitals. Such a
transformation can be formally written as
[ ˜φh
˜φp
]
=
[
Uh 0
0 Up
][
φh
φp
]
,
Uh and Up being unitary matrices mixing hole and particle states, res pectively. The
invariance follows simply from the fact that the energy is a s calar obtained from the
contraction of interaction and amplitude vertices, both of which (also the amplitude
equations) transforming as tensor components under the mix ing.
Spin-adaptation of the CC equations can be readily achieved by the UGA (see Sec.
2.1.7).
When dealing with closed-shell systems, there exists a simp ler, diagrammatic approach for
the CCSD equations. Both of these approaches will be discuss ed in Sec. 3.3.2 in the context
of MRCC.
Approximations of the CC model often provide a very good trad eoﬀ between accuracy and
conceptual-computational simplicity. Evaluation of the m ost "expensive" diagram of the
CCSD equations (the second one of Eq. (
2.3.9)) requires roughly O(n2
hn4
p) ∼ O (M 6) steps per
iteration (nh andnp being the number of hole and particle states), making the com putational
cost of CCSD similar to that of MBPT(3). The cost of non-linea r terms in the amplitude
equation can be reduced to that of linear terms by introducin g appropriate intermediate
quantities; see later in Sec.
3.3.3.
Inclusion of three-body excitations (CCSDT) would make the wave function correct through
second order and the energy through fourth order, leading to very accurate results. This
would, however, come at the price of a roughly O(M 8) computational cost, which is already
too much for general applications. For this reason, several approaches have been proposed
for approximate, less expensive treatment of T3, typically based on the analysis of MBPT
diagrams. Common choices include the CCSD[T] [ 73], CCSD(T) [ 74], CC3 [ 75] and iCCSDn
[76]. CCSD(T) has become perhaps the most popular one, often bei ng referred to as "gold
standard", oﬀering chemical accuracy ( ∼1 kcal mol −1 error) for an O(M 7) computational
cost. Recently, CCSD(T) level investigation of extremely l arge systems (containing hundreds
of atoms) has become possible [ 77–79].
Besides quantum chemistry, CC has also been investigated in the context of solid state
theory [ 80–82]. Generalizations of CC to ﬁnite temperature [ 83–85], to nuclear three-body
Hamiltonians [ 86, 87] and to relativistic settings [ 88, 89] are also known.
While CC is remarkably successful as a weak correlation mode l, it tends to fail in the
regime of strong correlation, where the Fermi vacuum is qual itatively diﬀerent from the
exact ground state (multiple covalent bond dissociation be ing a common example). In
such cases ﬁnding the converged solution to the amplitude eq uation proves to be hard
(often impossible), and even when found, the results are usu ally of poor quality. These
50
The Coupled Cluster model
problems can often be cured by alternative CC-like approach es preserving the concept of
Fermi vacuum, such as the Method of Moments CC of Piecuch et al . [
90], the Quadratic
CC of Van Voorhis and Head-Gordon [ 91], or the Extended CC of Arponen et al. [ 92–97].
Variational and unitary reformulations of CC [ 98–100] can be more robust against the
breakdown of reference function. Also, diﬀerent ﬂavours of variational CC can be related to
other electronic structure methods: variational CCS is equ ivalent to HF [ 101], while APSG
corresponds to an approximate variational CCSD [ 41].
In many cases, dissociation energy curves can be nicely cure d by a judicious exclusion of
spurious exchange contributions in the CC equations, as is d one in the Distinguishable
Cluster Approximation of Kats et al. [ 102–105].
Despite the often impressive success of modiﬁed single dete rminantal CC models in describing
strong correlation, the ultimate solution to the problem ap pears to be the generalization of
the formalism to an inherently MR wave function. In the follo wing section the literature of
MRCC approaches is brieﬂy reviewed.
2.3.4 Overwiew of Multi-Reference Coupled Cluster approac hes
In order to account for the missing static correlation plagu ing single determinantal CC, one
should apply the CC correction to a (typically variationall y optimized) linear combination
of Slater determinants:
|Φ⟩ =
∑
K∈µ
cK|ΦK⟩ . (2.3.20)
The determinants in the linear combination span the so-call ed model space, their indices
being collected in set µ. For practical reasons, one should try to keep the size of the model
space small. The model space of the APSG wave function (discu ssed in Sec.
2.2) contains
e.g.
|µAPSG | =
N/ 2∏
P =1
|GP |
determinants; for GVB this means |µGVB | ≤ 2N/ 2. The dynamic correlation can then be
introduced through an exponential wave operator resemblin g Eq. ( 2.3.2):
|Ψ⟩ = ˆΩ|Φ⟩ .
However, this is much more complicated than in the single det erminantal case, since many
diﬀerent forms of ˆΩ can be chosen, each of them deﬁning a possible MRCC model. Wh ile
the diﬀerent MRCC approaches often prove to be very eﬃcient a t combining strong and
weak correlations eﬀects, they also raise many new problems , both conceptual (treatment of
active orbitals, possible violation of size consistency an d unitary invariance) and practical
(redundancy of the parametrization, numerical instabilit ies).
Here we attempt to highlight some of the more common MRCC meth ods. Since the literature
of MRCC is quite extensive, only a brief and sketchy overview can be given here; see Refs.
[106–108] for a much more detailed discussion.
51
The Coupled Cluster model
Probably the ﬁrst MRCC Ansatz that saw routine applications is originating from Jeziorski
and Monkhorst [
109], and is deﬁned by the wave operator
ˆΩJM =
∑
K∈µ
exp(TK)|ΦK⟩⟨ ΦK| ,
which assigns diﬀerent exponential operators to each of the model space determinants,
strictly mapping them unto the complement of the model space . On one hand, this is
nice, since many useful (e.g. diagrammatic) techniques of s ingle determinantal CC can
be directly imported to facilitate the derivations (see e.g . Ref. [ 110]). On the other
hand, such a parametrization is inherently redundant: it co ntains roughly |µ| times more
parameters than the dimension of the CI space the exact wave f unction is expanded on.
The excitation operators can map diﬀerent model space deter minants unto the very same
excited determinant, meaning that the corresponding ampli tudes are linearly dependent.
This renders the Schrödinger equation by itself insuﬃcient in uniquely determining the
JM solution, and may lead to severe convergence problems. On e can ﬁx the amplitudes
either by using the JM Ansatz to calculate multiple electron ic states simultaneously (as
done in the state-universal approach), or by concentrating on a single state and making
the amplitudes well-deﬁned through additional constraint s (state-selective or state-speciﬁc
approach). The Mk-MRCC method of Mukherjee et al. [ 111–113] or the MRexpT method
of Hanrath [ 114, 115] are examples of the latter, as well as the variational MR-AQ CC of
Szalay and Bartlett [ 116]. Although in many cases highly accurate potential surface s can be
obtained from methods employing the JM Ansatz, the occurenc e of numerical instabilities
and the deterioration of results with the increasing number of active orbitals necessitated
the development of alternative MRCC parametrizations.
The so-called internally contracted (ic) state-speciﬁc MR CC methods use a (seemingly)
simpler wave operator with a single exponential operator ac ting on the whole model space:
ˆΩic = exp(T ) . (2.3.21)
This wave operator may lead to more compact equations than Ω JM . While it may not be as
explicit as for the JM Ansätze, ic-MRCC wave functions still suﬀer from linear dependence
of excitations; see Chapter
5.
Although the original idea of ic-MRCC can be traced back to th e seminal paper of Čížek [ 64],
development of models has gained momentum only later, the wo rks of Evangelista and
Gauss [117] and Hanauer and Köhn [ 118,119] being important examples. An eﬃcient unitary
ic-MRCC model (with T † = −T ) called canonical transformation theory has been worked ou t
by Yanai and Chan [ 120, 121], while an equation-of-motion ic-MRCC has been proposed by
Nooijen et al. for both ground and excited state calculation s [122–125]. The otherwise rather
complicated formalism of ic-MRCC can often be simpliﬁed by e xploiting the structure of the
underlying reference function (typically some geminal wav e function); examples include the
GVB-based block correlated CC of Li et al. [ 59] and the APSG-based linearized CC of Zoboki
et al. [ 54].
There is an important alternative form of ic-MRCC. As exp( T ) acts not on single
determinants but on the complete MR function, the concept of particles and holes must
be extended: occupied and active orbitals span the set of hol e states, while active and
52
The Coupled Cluster model
virtual orbitals span the set of particle states (see Sec.
2.1.2). Due to the appearance of
active orbitals these two sets are no longer disjoint, so nor mal ordering of the wave operator
now does make a diﬀerence. As an alternative to Eq. ( 2.3.21), one may thus choose
ˆΩic,N =:exp(T ): . (2.3.22)
Such an operator was considered ﬁrst probably by Lindgren in the context of open-shell
CC [ 126]. An ic-MRCC theory using Eq. ( 2.3.22) with the corresponding MRGNO and
MRGWT (see Secs. 2.1.5 and 2.1.6) was ﬁrst outlined by Mukherjee et al. [ 30, 34, 127].
Despite its many appealing properties (no contractions bet weenT operators, the possibility
of a manifestly connected formalism) the severe diﬃculties that arise during derivations
seriously limited the applicability of Eq. ( 2.3.22) Investigation of this ic-MRCC theory will
be the main subject of the forthcoming chapters.
The so-called valence-universal formalism [
128–132] presents a route to MRCC rather
diﬀerent from those above. Valence-universal models attem pt to treat the diﬀerent sectors
of the Fock-space (with diﬀerent number of electrons) on the same footing, and aim at a
uniﬁed description of excited, ionized and electron attach ed states. For this reason, they are
also called Fock-space methods; to distinguish, previousl y discussed MRCC theories (using
only the N-electron sector of the Fock-space) are often referred to as Hilbert-space methods.
Besides all of the "genuine" MRCC models listed above, much w ork has been put into the
development of MRCC methods resembling the traditional sin gle determinantal CC. These
methods are mostly based on the observation that the referen ce function can be written as
|Φ⟩ = (1 + Cint)|Φ0⟩ = exp(Tint)|Φ0⟩ ,
where |Φ0⟩ is a determinant (practically the one with the largest weigh t in the model space),
and action of the "internal wave operator" (1 + Cint) = exp(Tint) on |Φ0⟩ generates all other
|ΦK⟩ (for K ∈ µ). Combining this reference function with an "external wave operator"
generating excitations outside of the model space leads to a n almost single determinantal
CC parametrization: this is the starting point of the SRMRCC and CASCC developed
by Adamowicz et al. [ 133, 134]. A clever alternative approach based on quasi-particle
transformations was considered by Rolik and Kállay [ 135]. While often impressive accuracy
can be reached this way, the biggest drawback of almost singl e determinantal models
is the strong dependence of the results on the choice of |Φ0⟩ . Picking the determinant
with the largest weight is ambiguous in quasi-degenerate si tuations (such as covalent bond
dissociations). Also, unphysical discontinuities can app ear on potential surfaces wherever the
largest weight determinant changes, although the use of nat ural orbitals seems to dampen
this eﬀect [ 136].
As the above examples show, MRCC methods are capable of descr ibing a wide set of strongly
and weakly correlated phenomena, yet, there is still much to improve on. Hopefully, the next
chapters help to gain some further understanding of ic-MRCC .
53
The Coupled Cluster model

Chapter 3
A new look at internally contracted
Multi-Reference Coupled Cluster
A novel approach to ic-MRCC is presented in this chapter, usi ng the normal ordered
parametrization of Mukherjee et al. [
30, 34, 127] as a starting point. After setting up
the general formulae and demonstrating their connectednes s, diagrammatic methods are
found and used to construct the building blocks of the theory , dressed interaction vertices;
their interpretation and some important properties are rev iewed. This is followed by the
(also diagrammatic) derivation of the amplitude equation, after which the general ic-MRCC
formalism can be presented. Finally, a formally MBPT-like a nalysis of the equations, spin-
adaptation to singlet systems, as well as various approxima tion schemes are discussed.
The content of this chapter is based on Refs. [ 137, 138]. A precursor of this research was
presented earlier in Ref. [ 139].

as no explicit reference will be made to its exact structure t hroughout the derivations; when
it comes to actual calculations (see Chapter 6), a GVB reference will be used.

Note that the intermediate normalization (i.e. ⟨ Φ|Ψ⟩ = ⟨ :exp(T ):⟩ = 1) still holds due to

Despite the similar looking formulae, there are major diﬀer ences between the single
determinantal and the MR theory. The ﬁrst one is the appearan ce of active orbitals, giving
the sets of holes and particles (meant in the more general sen se of Sec. 2.3.4) a non-empty
intersection. In Eq. ( 3.1.1), indices i,j,k,l,... refer to occupied and active natural orbitals,
while indices a,b,c,d,... denote active and virtual natural orbitals. One can think of this
notation as simple bookkeeping to distinguish between anni hilated and created orbitals. For
the time being, no other restrictions are imposed on the exci tations entering Eq. ( 3.1.1).
Possible choices for the excitation manifold will be consid ered in Sec. 3.3.3. Also, in practice,
certain excitations will need to be removed from the Ansatz i n order to avoid numerical
instabilities; this will be discussed in Sec. 6.1.1.
Another important diﬀerence concerns the truncation of the wave operator when acting on
|Φ⟩ . In the single determinantal treatment of an N-electron system, the Taylor expansion
of :exp(T ): naturally ends after the N-th order. In general, however, this is not the case for
the MR theory, as normal ordered excitation operators with a ctive indices contain various
non-vanishing lower rank operators and even c-number valued terms (this can be seen by
e.g. inverting Eq. ( 2.1.41)). This makes the expansion of |Ψ⟩ non-terminating; nevertheless,
as long as CC amplitudes are assumed to be small (which holds i f |Φ⟩ provides a suﬃciently
good zeroth-order description), neglecting high order con tributions is justiﬁed.
Since |Ψ⟩ is an exact parametrization of the ground state, we can subst itute it in the
Schrödinger equation:
H|Ψ⟩ = ∆E|Ψ⟩ , (3.1.2)
again with ∆E =Eexact − ⟨H⟩ , ⟨ (...)⟩ referring to the expectation value calculated with |Φ⟩ .

quite diﬀerent from the one given above, and uses the concept of a similarity transformed

is known for :exp( T ):−1. We have simply avoided the use of this inverse, but there oth er
approaches actually tackling this problem [ 130].

3.2 Dressing of interaction vertices
The evaluation of Eqs. (
3.1.3) and ( 3.1.7) based on the MRGWT is much more challenging
than that of their single determinantal counterparts. Thes e equations are non-terminating
even for a truncated T -expansion, since any product of operators can be completel y
contracted with suﬃciently high rank cumulants; later, thi s will make further approximations
necessary. Any operator product will lead to much more compl icated results in the MR
case than in the single determinantal one due to the abundanc e of cumulants. It is too
easy to get lost in either the algebraic or the (naive) diagra mmatic derivation of the MRCC
equations. These derivations can be largely facilitated by the introduction of so-called dressed
interaction vertices.

The interpretation rules for ni1...niK ¯na1...¯naK ˜Wa1...a K
i1...i K can be stated as follows:
• V1: Write the product of algebraic expressions correspond ing to bare interaction and
cumulant vertices.
• V2: Assign a factor of ni or ¯na to each down- or upgoing non-cumulant line.
• V3: Sum over orbitals at each internal line.
• V4: Assign ( −1)H+C−L, with H, L and C denoting the number of downgoing
non-cumulant lines, loops (including quasi-loops) and the sum of cumulant ranks,
respectively.
• V5: Assign a symmetry factor of 1 /l! for each l-tuple of equivalent lines and 1 /v! for
eachv-tuple of equivalent diagram parts.
62
Dressing of interaction vertices
• V6: Antisymmetrize all external upper/lower indices that are not manifestly
antisymmetric.
These rules (in the context of ICSEK) were stated without proof. Here a short rationalization
is given for rules V4, V5 and V6, the only ones which do not foll ow immediately from the
MRGWT.
In order to see why the phase rule V4 is valid, note that outer i ndices and Hamiltonian
vertices imply "pairings" in the product: ˜ vqs
pr sets pairs pq and rs, while outer indices are
paired like ia, jb, and so on. Of course, interchanging indices (using antisym metry) would
lead to diﬀerent pairings.
Each diagram corresponds to a speciﬁc contraction pattern o f operators. This pattern can
always be brought to "maximal loop" form, in which upper-low er indices in the contraction
are moved near each other to resemble the aforementioned pai ring as much as possible. A
diagrammatic example of this rearrangement (taken from the third skeleton of Eq. (

the sign), and conclude that the phase factor must be ( −1)h−1. See Ref. [ 26] for a more
detailed discussion of this rule in the context of single det erminantal theories.
Another special case is h = 0, like the loops in Eq. ( 3.2.10) (with c = 1 in both cases). Note

Symmetry factors of rule V5 follow from the antisymmetry of i nteraction vertices and

current case l = 2 is the only possibility, since the Hamiltonian is a two-bo dy operator at

later.

More generally, let us assume a diagram having v equivalent parts with (say) m lower
indices assigned to each of them. Let us further assume that u pper indices are completely
antisymmetric (either by default, or by a previous antisymm etrization). Lower indices
must be antisymmetrized with A(i1...i m)... (ivm−m+1...i vm), the parantheses serving as a reminder
that only indices between separate quantities are permuted (cf. the deﬁnition of A in
Appendix A). This generates (vm)!/(m!)v terms with all unique terms counted v! times. This
overcounting stems from interchanging ( ik1...ikm) and (il1...ilm) between equivalent diagram
parts. The role of symmetry factor 1 /v! in rule V5 is simply to correct this redundancy.
Again, in the current case, the two-body nature of the Hamilt onian limits the possible
number of equivalent diagram parts to v = 2.
