Dressing of interaction vertices
Dressed vertices with arbitrary orientation of legs can thu s always be evaluated using the
original particle-hole vertex with appropriate barred ind ices and substitutions n¯a = −¯na,
¯n¯i = −ni. The form of Eq. (
3.2.14) ensures that dressed and bare vertices appear with the
same sign, as claimed before.
The main conclusion is that all variants of a dressed vertex a re represented by essentially
the same diagrams, with only the leg orientations being diﬀe rent. Interpretation rules of
the previous section have to be only marginally modiﬁed to ac count for the diﬀerent factors
associated with legs.
It is somewhat cumbersome to evaluate all other variants of a vertex starting from the
particle-hole one through substitutions. Fortunately, th is dependence on the particle-hole
vertex can be eliminated, and all variants can be treated on t he same footing after introducing
"neutral" factors

Interpretation of dressed vertices is probably the most tra nsparent in real space coordinate

A few simple, yet important properties of dressed vertices a re to be discussed.
Dressed vertices are antisymmetric under permutation of up per or lower indices; for example
˜Vak
ic = − ˜V ka
ic = − ˜Vak
ci = ˜Vka
ci .
Particle-hole dressed vertices vanish identically whenev er reference function |Φ⟩ happens to
be an exact eigenstate of H, since in such cases H|Φ⟩ = |∅⟩ . This is a desirable property,
as it guarantees that many-body methods employing these ver tices do not add any spurious
69
The general ic-MRCC formalism
correction to the already exact solution via Eq. (
3.2.1). Note however, that this vanishing
can only happen if the vertex is evaluated exactly, and no cum ulants are neglected based
on e.g. rank. This highlights the danger of neglecting EPV cu mulants on an ad-hoc basis
(see Sec. 2.1.4). Consider a two-electron system with exact ground state |Φ⟩ (provided
by e.g. APSG if all orbitals are assigned to the geminal): in t his case, three- and four-
body cumulants in Eqs. ( 3.2.6) and ( 3.2.7) are EPV, but dropping them would violate the
vanishing of vertices. Besides the plausible reasoning of H anauer and Köhn [ 13], this is the
main argument why EPV cumulants are always to be kept.
Dressed interaction vertices transform as tensor componen ts under unitary rotations in the
subspace of core or virtual orbitals. This simply follows fr om their structure, and the fact
that working in the natural basis does not aﬀect core and virt ual orbitals. For example, let
us consider the particle-hole type one-body dressed vertex withφi being a core orbital (with
ni = 1):
¯naFa
i = ¯nafa
i − 1
2
∑
p,q,r
˜vrp
iq ,
all other terms of Eq. ( 3.2.4) vanishing in accordance with Sec. 2.1.4; correct transformation
properties in the core or virtual subspace then follow from t he transformation properties of
bare vertices. This means that the logic of Sec. 2.3.3 applies, and the ic-MRCC energy
calculated with Eq. ( 3.2.1) is invariant under rotations in the core or virtual subspac e. Note
that in general, no such rotational freedom is present for ac tive orbitals, as they are ﬁxed
by the requirement of being natural.
Intersystem cumulants vanish for inﬁnitely separated sing let subsystems, as it was discussed
in Sec.
2.1.4. One can assume that all orbitals are localized on subsystem s in such a case,
hence intersystem bare vertices also vanish. These two prop erties combined with the manifest
connectedness of the equations make all intersystem dresse d vertices disappear. This feature
ensures size consistency over singlet subsystems for any co rrection method built with dressed
vertices. The harder question of size consistency over non- trivially spin coupled subsystems
will not be addressed here.
Finally note that dressed vertices for singlet systems are i nvariant under the action of spin
rotation group SU (2) (since both bare vertices and cumulants are invariant), and so the
results of Appendix
C are applicable. We will make use of this during the singlet sp in-
adaptation of ic-MRCC (see Sec. 3.3.2).
3.3 The general ic-MRCC formalism
Having established the concept of dressed interaction vert ices in the previous sections, the
ic-MRCC equations can ﬁnally be derived. In order not to get l ost in details, we present the
derivations in the CCSD approximation (i.e. T =T1 +T2); generalizing the results to higher
excitation levels is straightforward.

CK ′(τ ) standing for contractions between : aK: and :aL: altogether of rank K ′, and index τ

two, third and fourth lines correspond to K ′ = 2, 1, 0, respectively. The number of topologies
(skeleton diagrams) associated with them is three, one and o ne, in the same order.
Based on the above, construction rules of diagrams contribu ting to the amplitude equation
are relatively simple to formulate with dressed interactio n vertices. Essentially the same rules
and notations are to be used as in Secs. 2.3.2 and 3.2.1. One must draw all topologically
distinct connected diagrams with 2 K external legs, using one dressed interaction vertex and
any number of amplitudes and cumulants. Self-contraction o f any vertex is to be avoided,
just like any direct cumulant-cumulant or cumulant-dresse d interaction vertex contraction.

The ic-MRCC interpretation rules are essentially identica l to the ones derived in Secs. 3.2.1
and 3.2.2. They are summarized here:
• A1: Write the product of algebraic expressions correspond ing to dressed interaction,
cumulant and amplitude vertices.
• A2: Assign a factor of ni or ¯na to each down- or upgoing non-cumulant line.
• A3: Sum over orbitals at each internal line.
• A4: Assign ( −1)H+C−L, with H, L and C denoting the number of downgoing
non-cumulant lines, loops (including quasi-loops) and the sum of cumulant ranks,
respectively.
• A5: Assign a symmetry factor of 1 /l! for each l-tuple of equivalent lines and 1 /v! for
eachv-tuple of equivalent diagram parts.
• A6: Antisymmetrize all external upper/lower indices that are not manifestly
antisymmetric.

during the iteration process. In order to avoid ambiguities , the vertical position of cumulant
vertices must be ﬁxed: in each iteration, they must be placed as high in the diagram as
possible without breaking the ordering of interaction vert ices. Substituting the above forms
oft1 andt2 in Eq. ( 3.3.8) leads to a similar expansion of the energy correction. The d iagrams
are interpreted similarly to the ones of the ic-MRCC equatio ns, with three further rules:
• P1: assign a factor of 1 /Di1...i K
a1...a K to all ﬁctitious (dotted) horizontal lines between
interaction vertices (above the respective cumulant vertices) crossingK pairs of fermion
lines.
• P2: restrict the summation over internal lines, so that i ̸= k for Fi
k and a ̸= c for Fc
a
in the numerator.
• P3: in the case of 2 K external lines with labels i1,...,i K,a 1,...,a K, divide the term by
ni1...niK ¯na1 ¯naK .

The above expressions are reminiscent of the MBPT expansion of single determinantal CC
amplitudes (hence of the wave function) and of the energy (cf . Eqs. ( 2.3.17), ( 2.3.18) and
(2.3.19)). For example, Eq. ( 3.3.16) is just like the MBPT(2) energy, with dressed vertices
instead of bare ones. While the analogy is clearly formal − none of these expansions are
backed up by any partitioning of H −, it is still instructive to talk about the series in a
"pseudo-perturbative" sense. Diagrams containing n dressed interaction vertices are said to
be of order n.
One of the new features of this expansion compared with MBPT i s the appearance of
arbitrary K-body vertices. In general, all tK contain a ﬁrst-order diagram due to ˜WK,
hence, they can all contribute to the second-order correcti on of ∆ E. In a CCSD scheme,
Eqs. ( 3.3.14) and ( 3.3.15) show all diagrams contributing to t1 and t2 up to second order.
At variance with the single determinantal theory, inclusio n of higher excitations would bring
further terms.
The other unusual feature of the expansion is the explicit ap pearance of cumulants connecting
interaction vertices. We can see that strong electron corre lation encoded in cumulants
manifests itself in two ways: on one hand it provides dressin g for bare one- and two-body
vertices and creates new K-body vertices for K ≥ 3, while on the other hand it also provides
couplings between these vertices beyond mean-ﬁeld level pa ir-contractions. This observation
leads us to partition strong correlation into intravertex ( implicit) and intervertex (explicit)
parts. This interpretation is also in agreement with the sca ttering picture assigned to dressed
vertices and cumulants in Appendix
D. From a more practical point of view, thinking with
intra- and intervertex correlation may help in developing a pproximation schemes for the
general ic-MRCC theory (see Sec. 3.3.3).

• S5: Assign a factor of 2 L′

• S6: Symmetrize upper-lower indices ia and jb.

In principle, the two routes to singlet spin-adaptation out lined above are equivalent, and one
is free to choose between Eqs. (
3.3.17) and ( 3.3.18). Transparent diagrammatic formulation
is an obvious advantage of the latter. However, in practice, solving the amplitude equations
is not that simple, since linear dependence of the projectin g excited states must be treated as
well. The handling of this redundancy turns out to be more str aightforward in the UGA-like
scheme, where the overlap of spin-summed states : Ea1...a K
i1...i K : |Φ⟩ is to be examined. For this
reason, we are adopting a "hybrid" approach: in the next sect ion, we will derive spin-free
approximations to ic-MRCC using diagrams, and with Eq. ( 3.3.18) in mind. These equations
are then converted to UGA formulae with the help of Eq. ( 3.3.19). See Chapter 5 for details
on the treatment of redundancy.
3.3.3 Approximations to the general formalism
The usefulness of the general theory outlined in previous se ctions is seriously hindered
by the extreme number of diagrams even in the CCSD scheme: non e of the above ic-
MRCC equations are truncated at any ﬁnite order of T . Diagrammatically speaking, this is
81
The general ic-MRCC formalism
because an arbitrary number of amplitude vertices can be con nected to suﬃciently high rank
cumulants and dressed vertices, while still leaving 2 K legs free. Thinking of the discussion
of Sec.
2.1.4, in general, we cannot say that cumulants become negligible at a rank that
would allow for substantial simpliﬁcation of the equations . It is the assumption on the small
value of the amplitudes that provides a basis for arguing the neglect of higher orders in the
wave operator.
In this section, a couple of approximation schemes are propo sed for ic-MRCCSD. Latin
indices again denote spatial orbitals.
Based on Sec.
3.3.1, our approximations can go along basically two paths: negle cting
intravertex or intervertex correlation eﬀects. The ﬁrst op tion can be reached by leaving out
diagrams containing high rank dressed interaction vertice s. Since the Hamiltonian contains
only one- and two-body parts, it seems reasonable to keep ver tices F and ˜V only, while
discarding all ˜WK forK ≥ 3. Examples of omitted diagrams include the fourth and the la st
two of diagrams of Eq. ( 3.3.7), and all but the ﬁrst three diagrams of Eq. ( 3.3.8).
While this approximation based on a Hamiltonian analogy is p lausible, it is nevertheless an
assumption that we cannot prove in a rigorous way. We can argu e that the direct eﬀect of
odd-rank dressed vertices on the energy is small. This is sim ply due to the fact that each
of the odd rank dressed vertices must be connected to at least one t1 in Eq. ( 3.3.8), whose
eﬀect can be safely considered small (given that orbitals of |Φ⟩ are optimized, which is the
typical case). Some further rationalization for the GVB wav e function is given in Sec. 6.1.
Note that indirect eﬀect of high rank vertices on the energy ( coming from their contributions
to amplitudes) cannot be estimated this way.
From a certain point of view, neglecting some diagrams with h igh rank vertices is actually
recommended to avoid unphysical results. Let us show on a sim ple example why one must
be cautious with inconsistent approximations regarding dr essed vertices. In the general ic-
MRCCSD theory, we could obtain a third-order correction to t he energy by substituting the
leading order contributions of t1 and t2 to the third diagram of Eq. (
3.3.8):
/A1
→
/A2
×
.
Note that in a general setting using complex spin orbitals, t his energy diagram is complex
valued. Its unphysical imaginary part is cancelled by its co njugate diagram, which, however,
can only be introduced via triple excitations:
/A1
→
/A2
→
/A3
×
.
In a CCSD scheme, keeping the three-body vertex in the energy formula could thus generate
a spurious complex valued energy correction. Even when usin g real orbitals, keeping this
vertex would correspond to keeping only one half of the respe ctive energy term, which seems
82
The general ic-MRCC formalism
rather ad-hoc. It might be better to drop the three-body vert ex from the energy formula.
To be fair, this is not just an MR issue: one could obtain such s trange results even in single
determinantal CC if unbalanced approximations were introd uced (non-variational CCS being
a simple example).
Unfortunately, the general amplitude equations remain quite untractable even after excluding
high rank dressed vertices. In the next step of approximatio ns, some of the intervertex
correlation must be neglected. The simplest possible choic e is to drop this part of correlation
completely (thus erasing cumulant couplings between dress ed vertices), and treat only
intravertex correlation hidden within F and ˜V . While this approximation (to be referred
to as idCCSD after interaction dressing) is rather crude, it is quite appealing from a formal
point of view. It provides a natural truncation for the ampli tude equations.
With explicitly cumulant-dependent diagrams removed, all remaining spin orbital diagrams
formally resemble those of single determinantal CC (see Eqs . (
2.3.7) and ( 2.3.8)), the only
diﬀerence being the replacement of bare vertices by their dr essed counterparts. Algebraic
form of the equations can then be easily found; spin free form of Ωi
a and Ωij
ab can also be
directly obtained with the diagrammatic techniques of the p revious section. The UGA form
of the spin-free idCCSD equations read

Chapter 4
Calculation of dispersion interactions
In this chapter, certain approximation schemes of ic-MRCC a re examined in the context
of dispersion interactions. Investigations are carried ou t for closed-shell electrically neutral
subsystems separated by a large distance R. Following a brief overview of the literature on
standard calculation techniques, the emergence of the Lond on dispersion formula
Edisp. ≈ −C6
R6 (4.0.1)
is demonstrated for the second-order idCCSD energy. In this approximation, we also show
that dispersion coeﬃcient C6 can be expressed in terms of dressed quantities formally
resembling the frequency-dependent polarizabilites (FDP ) known from the analogous single
determinantal theory. Finally, the general Edisp ∼ −1/R6 decay is argued to be valid for the
complete idCCSD, as well as for the general ic-MRCC formalis m.
Latin indices are reserved for spatial orbitals in this chap ter; spin indices are shown explicitly
whenever they are needed.
This chapter is based on Ref. [
147].
4.1 Overview
Dispersion interaction arises from the charge density ﬂuct uations of the subsystems, leading
to weakly bound states between e.g. closed-shell partners e ven if they lack permanent
electric moments [
12, 148–150]. The importance of dispersion interaction in both chemica l
and biological systems cannot be overemphasized.
For two subsystems at a large separation distance R, the asymptotic dispersion energy in
leading order is given by Eq. (
4.0.1) provided that R is not large enough for electromagnetic
retardation eﬀects to become noticeable. The dispersion co eﬃcient C6 can be expressed in
terms of the FDP of each subsystem. Providing an equally accu rate description of dispersion
both in the equilibrium and in the asymptotic regime has rema ined a challenge for electronic
structure methods to this day.
89
Derivation of the London formula in the leading order of idCC SD
Mean-ﬁeld methods cannot even qualitatively account for di spersion; such phenomena are
usually introduced via correlation corrections, obtained , for example by MBPT. Szabo and
Ostlund showed [ 151] that a supermolecular MBPT(2) calculation gives rise to di spersion
energy exhibiting the correct asymptotic behaviour (i.e. c onforming Eq. ( 4.0.1)), but with
C6 calculated only at the level of so-called uncoupled HF [ 152]. An alternative of the
supermolecule approach is the Symmetry-Adapted Perturbat ion Theory (SAPT) of Jeziorski
et al. (see Ref. [ 153] and references cited therein), in which the intersystem in teraction is
treated as perturbation. The relationship between supermo lecular MBPT and SAPT has
been investigated in detail [ 154].
The quality of the supermolecular approach can be improved b y applying corrections more
involved than MBPT(2), e.g. the all-order inclusion of dire ct ring diagrams (and possibly
certain kinds of exchange ring diagrams) [ 71]. The eﬀect of these diagrams can be easily
taken into account in RPA. Diﬀerent variants of RPA (disting uished by the diﬀerent degree
of including exchange diagrams) improve quantitatively on the MBPT(2) results, but not
all of them lead to a correct expression for the dispersion co eﬃcient [151,155]. Another way
to include higher-order diagrams in the energy is through CC , of course. CCSD(T) yields
essentially exact results for intermolecular interaction s and is often used as benchmark in
such cases. The CC model also provides a way to sum up only ring diagrams (as was shown
in Sec. 3.3.3 for the MR case), leading to e.g. the rCCD approach [ 72], a computationally
less demanding alternative to CCSD(T). The formal equivale nce of certain kinds of RPA and
rCCD methods has been demonstrated on many occasions [ 63,71,72,156]. Most RPA/rCCD-
like methods do not give a satisfactory description of long r ange dispersion interactions
[151, 157]: while they produce the correct algebraic decay, the dispe rsion coeﬃcient C6 can
rarely be expressed in terms of FDP-s.
Even in MR methods, dispersion eﬀects are typically introdu ced in a second step via
correction methods. For example, strongly orthogonal gemi nal approaches alone (geminals
being localized on subsystems) are no better at describing s uch phenomena than mean-ﬁeld
methods; however, quite accurate results can be obtained wi th linearized CC [
54] or RPA-
type [ 57, 58] corrections. Therefore, it is still interesting to invest igate MR methods in the
context of dispersion energy calculations, as will be done i n the next section.
4.2 Derivation of the London formula in the leading
order of idCCSD

shell, electrically neutral atoms A and B, separated at a large distance R (large enough to
neglect overlap of charge clouds). Orbitals are assumed to b e localized on either A or B.

The above expressions are formally similar to the well known formulae of single determinantal
theory [ 151], the main diﬀerence being that quantities like µa
i , χ(r, r′,iω ) or α (iω) are all
cumulant-dependent. Although the derivations were carrie d out for atoms, similar results
could be found for non-spherical subsystems (after suitabl e rotational averaging). The
cumulant corrections to the otherwise mean-ﬁeld-like quan tities possibly make the model
more suitable for describing dispersion interaction betwe en strongly correlated systems.
Following the same arguments, one could show that intersyst em components of higher rank
particle-hole dressed vertices Wi1...i K
a1...a K fall oﬀ at least as 1/R3 too, similarly to Eq. (
4.2.6).
Since these vertices provide the leading-order contributi ons to amplitudes through the CC
iteration, one can conclude that intersystem amplitudes al so fall oﬀ at least as 1/R3, leading
to the Edisp ∼ −1/R6 decay of dispersion energy in the general ic-MRCC framework as well.
Whether one can extract a closed form expression for C6 strongly depends on the underlying
approximation schemes.
We mention that when dealing with more than two subsystems, h igher-order diagrams
include pair-wise non-additive dispersion eﬀects as well. The simplest example is given
by the third-order direct ring diagram for three well-separ ated subsystems A, B and C (cf.

Finally, let us comment on the interpretation (or interpret ability) of the obtained results.
Cumulant dressing of quantities like µa
i , or α (iω) goes well with the standard interpretation
of dispersion interaction. The usual explanation is as foll ows [
12]: Eq. ( 4.2.9) describes
Coulomb interactions between points r1, r′
1 and r2, r′
2 of atoms A and B, respectively,
propagation of density ﬂuctuations on the separate atoms be ing described by response
functions χA(r1, r′
1,iω ) and χB(r2, r′
2,iω ). Taking Eq. ( 4.2.1) into account, we see that
dispersion arises from the interaction of induced electric moments generated by these density
disturbances. The very role of cumulant dressing is to provi de corrections to the otherwise
mean-ﬁeld level propagation of density ﬂuctuations.
On a formal level, identifying Eq. (
4.2.10) as a response function checks out: it describes
density disturbances mediated by single excitations manif esting themselves not just via pair-
contractions but cumulants as well. On the other hand, this a nalogy has limitations, as shown

below.

Chapter 5
Treating the linear dependence of the
excitation manifold
During the derivation of the ic-MRCC formalism in Chapter
3, we included all kinds of
excitations in the parametrization of T and in the projection equations without considering
the possible linear dependence of these excited states. Unl ike in a single determinantal theory,
automatic inclusion of all possible excitations involving active orbitals leads to a redundant
set of vectors {: EK : |Φ ⟩} . This is a general problem of ic-MRCC, as one may encounter
serious numerical instabilities when trying to solve an ill -posed system of equations.
Redundancy is usually handled by elimination of certain amp litudes or linear combinations
thereof. An unfortunate consequence of amplitude eliminat ion is the possible appearance of
discontinuities on the Potential Energy Surface (PES). Int erference with orbital invariance
and size consistency has been reported for some of the redund ancy treatments investigated
in Ref. [ 118], with size extensivity restored in a follow-up work [ 119]. To avoid such pitfalls,
the use of so-called many-body residual equations is often a dvocated instead of projection
equations [ 130].
In the present chapter, a hitherto unexplored scheme is intr oduced for handling redundancy
when CC amplitude equations are obtained as ordinary projec tions (as was done in Sec.
3.1). We rely on the concept of so-called frames which facilitat es keeping all the amplitudes
corresponding to the redundant set of excited functions. Fr ames are generalizations of basis
sets, relaxing the requirement of linear independence on th e constituting vectors. They
were originally introduced in connection with signal analy sis, and have found ever increasing
applications in physics, engineering and computer science [158, 159].
In this chapter we ﬁrst show the emergence of linear dependen ce on a simple example. This
is followed by the general procedure to solve the redundant s ystem of amplitude equations
without removing excitations. Finally, this procedure is a lso demonstrated using a simple
example.
Spatial orbitals are used throughout this chapter, denoted by latin indices.
This chapter is based on Ref. [
138].
97

Let us introduce the compact notation of {|ψµ ⟩} for the set of general excited states (index
µ running over all singly and doubly excited states). Then the overlap matrix can be written
as
Sµν = ⟨ψµ |ψν ⟩ .
Zero eigenvalues of this matrix signal redundancy among the excited states: the amplitude
equation contains less information than the number of param eters. Among such

Extracting unique amplitudes from the redundant amplitude equation
circumstances the amplitude vector t, collecting elements tµ (assigned to excitation |ψµ ⟩ ), is
ill deﬁned.
Indeed, it is not hard to ﬁnd an example of such singular behav iour. Let us consider a GVB
wave function with orbitals φp and φq assigned to the same geminal (so that np +nq = 1 ).
Furthermore, let φi be a core orbital, while φa is virtual. Due to the strongly orthogonal
structure of GVB, the overlap matrix takes a simple block-di agonal form (this will be used
in Chapter
6). It is not hard to see that so-called exchange spectator-ty pe excitations
|ψ1⟩ =:Eap
pi : |Φ ⟩ and |ψ2⟩ =:Eaq
qi : |Φ ⟩ form a two-by-two block of S with a zero eigenvalue:
S2×2 = 2npnq

]

hence, they are linearly dependent (actually, |ψ1⟩ = −|ψ2⟩ ). The above matrix can be
obtained either with the help of Eqs. ( 5.1.1) and ( 2.2.8), or by calculating the action of

In order to keep the argument simple, we do not consider direc t spectator-type excitations
: Epa
pi : |Φ ⟩ and : Eqa
qi : |Φ ⟩ . In general, these would couple to |ψ1⟩ and |ψ2⟩ , generating a
four-by-four block.
The zero eigenvalue of S2×2 means that − although looking diﬀerent on the surface − vectors
|ψ1⟩ and |ψ2⟩ (or an arbitrary linear combination of them) represent the v ery same state,
and, in principle, no information would be lost by discardin g either one of them. However,
in general, this could lead to undesired side eﬀects, as ment ioned above. To prevent this, we
proceed to ﬁnd a way to solve the amplitude equation for the re dundant vectors without the
need to remove any of them.
5.2 Extracting unique amplitudes from the redundant

Before dealing with the amplitude equation, certain sets of vectors (and their associated dual

In general, diagonalization of S and inversion of α could be quite demanding computation-
wise. Note however, that in practice (for example, when usin g a strongly orthogonal geminal
reference), the overlap matrix admits a block-diagonal for m, and both the diagonalization
and the redundancy handling can be done independently for ea ch (typically small) block.

Chapter 6
Computation of Potential Energy
Surfaces
This chapter deals with some practical aspects of the approx imate ic-MRCC models
idCCSD, idCCSD+λF, rmCCD and rCCD(UGA), all of them having been introduced in
Sec.
3.3.3. We present the details of ic-MRCC calculations built on the GVB wave function.
Computational beneﬁts of choosing GVB as reference functio n are discussed in detail, just
like the hardships associated with our ic-MRCC calculation s (convergence issues, and the
need to remove certain excitations from the Ansatz).
This is followed by numerical illustrations involving Pote ntial Energy Surface (PES)
calculations to test the capability of the ic-MRCC approxim ations in describing strongly and
weakly correlated systems. The examples investigated incl ude breaking of covalent bonds,
conformational changes, deprotonation processes and disp ersion interactions. Strengths and
weaknesses of the approximation schemes are discussed.
In this chapter, latin indices represent spatial orbitals.
This chapter is based on Refs. [
137, 138, 147].
6.1 Computational details
In practice, all of our ic-MRCC corrections are built on the G VB wave function (see Sec.
2.2.1). GVB calculations start from restricted HF orbitals local ized to bonds or atoms
using the Foster-Boys procedure [ 160]. These localized spatial orbitals are then assigned to
geminals. Two spatial orbitals are assigned to all valence g eminals corresponding to bonding
and lone electron pairs. On the other hand, core geminals des cribing inner-shell electrons
are kept on the HF level and get only one spatial orbital. All o ther orbitals not assigned to
any of the geminals are virtual.
The GVB procedure optimizes orbitals and geminal coeﬃcient s simultaneously. The
resulting active orbitals (belonging to valence geminals) are chosen to be natural orbitals
diagonalizing the 1-RDM. Core and virtual orbitals remain t o be the very same localized HF
107
Computational details
orbitals we started with and are natural orbitals by constru ction (with eigenvalues ni = 1
and na = 0, respectively).
Let the number of core, active and virtual spatial orbitals b e denoted by mc, ma and mv,
respectively, with the total number of spatial orbitals giv en by ¯M =mc +ma +mv.
Using the results of Sec.
2.2.2, cumulants of the GVB wave function can be constructed
in a straightforward manner. At ﬁrst, storing these cumulan ts appears to be an obstacle,
since a K-body cumulant is composed of m2K
a elements; when dealing with a large molecule
having many active orbitals, this number could be extremely large even for relatively low-
rank cumulants. This problem, however, is easy to overcome, as the strongly orthogonal
nature of GVB makes cumulants very sparse: all of their 2K indices must belong to the
same geminal, otherwise they vanish. This makes the maximal number of non-zero entries
22K per geminal, hence 22KN/2 = 2 2K−1N for the complete wave function. Furthermore,
GVB cumulants are identically zero for K >4 (see Sec. 2.2.2). Therefore, GVB cumulants
are quite easy to store as long as the number of geminals does n ot become too high.
Having obtained the necessary cumulants, dressed interact ion vertices are to be constructed
in the next step. Similarly to cumulants, dressed vertices n eed to be calculated only once
before starting the CC iteration. Each variant of a K-body dressed vertex (corresponding
to the diﬀerent leg orientations) is an array containing rou ghly ¯M 2K elements. The actual
number of elements is smaller when excluding identically ze ro entries (e.g. Vak
ic has no more
than (mc +ma)2(ma +mv)2 non-zero elements); exploiting permutational symmetry co uld
lead to further reduction.
Calculation of dressed vertices could become quite costly w ith a general MR function; as an
example, let us concentrate on the two-body dressed vertex. Looking at Eq. (
3.2.18), we
can see that terms involving the four-body cumulant or a prod uct of two-body cumulants
are the most demanding computation-wise, requiring roughl y ¯M 4n4
a steps to build. Sparsity
of GVB cumulants greatly reduces this number, as all indices belonging to a cumulant must
run over the same geminal. In the case of the two-body vertex, this renders the number
of steps to build the cumulant dressing at most 27N, meaning that dressing terms can be
added quite inexpensively to bare vertices.
In the next step, the overlap matrix S needs to be constructed and diagonalized for all spin-
summed excitations of the GVB function that we plan to use for the ic-MRCC correction
(see Sec.
6.1.1). Due to the strong orthogonality of GVB, S is block-diagonal, and one
can investigate the small blocks separately. Excited state s are to be classiﬁed based on the
eigenvalues of these blocks; there are three possible cases .
The simplest case is when none of the block eigenvalues are ze ro: this means that all of the
constituent excited states are unique, and no further redun dancy treatment is needed. During
the iterative solution of the amplitude equation, amplitud es assigned to these excitations can
be updated with the usual technique of Sec. 3.3.1.
If some of the block eigenvalues are zero then there is redund ancy in the states, and the
corresponding amplitudes are to be updated with the techniq ue of Sec. 5.2.
Finally, the special case of all block eigenvalues being zer o signals that excited states of the
block are actually zero vectors. These states can be discard ed, as the procedure of Sec. 5.2
would assign a zero amplitude to all of them anyway. This is al so the reason why the results
108
Computational details
do not depend on the block structure of S: one could in principle append any block with
additional zero states, as the redundancy treatment would a lways make their amplitudes
vanish. In practice, however, it is recommended to discard z ero states, as division by very
small numbers may introduce numerical instabilities.
Of course, in order to classiﬁy states the above way, a numeri cal threshold must be set up
to distinguish between zero and non-zero eigenvalues. A thr eshold of Σ min = 10 −10 is used
uniformly for all rCCD(UGA) calculations, while calculati ons done with rmCCD, idCCSD
and idCCSD+λF employ Σ min = 10 −9. In the latter three cases, a second threshold of
Σ block = 10 −8 was set for the trace of overlap blocks: all states are discar ded if the trace of
their block falls below Σ block.
Scaling as O((nc +na)3(na +nv)3), solution of the approximate ic-MRCCSD equations has
roughly the same computational cost per iteration as single determinantal CCSD. However,
in most cases, making the solution of amplitude equations co nverge is not straightforward:
poor choice of control parameters may lead to very slow conve rgence or even divergence of
the iteration. While the MBPT-like energy denominator of Eq . (
3.3.12) seems to be the
best from an interpretational point of view, in practice it i s much better to use the diagonal
part of the complete coeﬃcient matrix (in the given approxim ation scheme) to update the
non-redundant amplitudes. In redundant blocks, amplitude s are updated via inversion of
the whole coeﬃcient matrix, as discussed in Sec. 5.2. In the strongly correlated parts of the
PES, shifting the control parameters by a small constant val ue often improves convergence.
In order to achieve convergence with the rCCD(UGA) and rmCCD methods, an additional
scaling of the control parameters is required.
6.1.1 Pruning the excitation manifold
Up to this point, we have not discussed the necessity to omit c ertain excitations from the
parametrization of T ; we have simply included all possible excitations from core /active
orbitals to active/virtual orbitals. Sadly, this approach might run into trouble if we try to
excite either from very weakly occupied orbitals (with ni ≪ 1) or to very strongly occupied
orbitals (with ¯na ≪ 1).
The reason can be understood by analyzing the leading order c ontributions to amplitudes

110
Computational details
instabilities during the CC iteration, endangering the con vergence of the procedure. In
order to avoid this, we discard these excited states from the ic-MRCC Ansatz. Note that the
ill-eﬀect of excitations changes with the change of occupat ion factors along the PES: during
the dissociation of a covalent bond, strongly and weakly occ upied orbitals of a GVB geminal
become quasi-degenerate in occupation, and the problem ass ociated with e.g. Eq. (
6.1.2)
diminishes. This means that in the dissociation limit, cert ain excitations could be allowed
which are forbidden around equilibrium geometry. But this w ould potentially introduce
discontinuities along the PES in points where the number of e xcitations changes; therefore,
it is best to discard problematic excitations uniformly alo ng the dissociation curve.
By examining the other terms of one- and two-body dressed ver tices similary to the above, it
is not hard to identify potentially dangerous excited state s, hence deﬁne excitation selection
schemes for our ic-MRCC approximations.
The rCCD(UGA) calculations are carried out with the followi ng restrictions on the excitation
manifold {:Eab

• ni¯na> 1/4 if i ̸=a and I =A,

• nj ¯nb > 1/4 if j ̸=b and J =B,
• ni > 1/2 if i =j,

6.1.2 High-rank dressed vertices in GVB
In Sec.
3.3.3, we argued that the inclusion of high-rank dressed vertices in the energy formula
might not lead to a balanced MRCCSD formalism. Nevertheless , it would be important to
have at least an estimation about the eﬀect of these neglecte d dressed vertices on the energy.
The simple structure of GVB cumulants makes it possible to ca rry out such an analysis in
a special case.
In this section, spin orbital dressed vertices ˜WK are used instead of spin free vertices WK.
Spin indices are shown explicitily.
Let us consider the GVB description of a system with only two a ctive spatial orbitals (that is,
four spin orbitals with indices pα,pβ,qα,qβ ; let us choose np ≥nq). A basic example would
be a molecule with only one correlated bond, all other electr on pairs being treated on the
HF level (that is, their geminals contain only one spatial or bital). Furthermore, let us apply
the stricter excitation selection scheme devised for idCCS D, idCCSD+λF and rmCCD.
It is not hard to see that almost all energy contributions ass ociated with high-rank vertices
vanish in this setting. The reasons can be summed up as follow s. Cumulant terms of
the dressed vertex can only take values from the one geminal w ith active orbitals, that is,
the only labels that can be assigned to cumulant legs are pα,pβ,qα,qβ . This means that
vertex diagrams may have at most four incoming and four outgo ing cumulant legs, otherwise
they vanish by antisymmetry. Finally, only those vertex dia grams can contribute to the
energy which can be connected to amplitudes of allowed excit ations (that is, excitations not
forbidden by the selection scheme of the previous section). Vertices of rank ﬁve or higher
(having at least seven cumulant legs) cannot conform to all o f these conditions. In the
four-body vertex, one may ﬁnd a diagram that satisﬁes all the above criteria:
/A1
aαiα bβ qβ qαpα qβpβ
→
/A2
,
and another one obtained by reversing the orientation of fer mion lines. However, this diagram
is zero in GVB, since λqαqβpβ
pαpβqβ = 0 is the only cumulant contributing to it. The vanishing
of this cumulant can be easily seen: based on Appendix
C, it can be written as a linear
combination of spin-summed cumulants Λ qqp
ppq and Λ qqp
pqp, both of which are zero based on Eq.
(
2.2.11).
The three-body vertex may contribute to the energy through t he fourth and ﬁfth skeletons
of Eq. ( 3.2.9). However, as discussed in Sec. 3.3.3, its eﬀect on the energy is limited, as it
must be contracted to at least one t1 (which is expected to be small).
112
Some numerical results
The above considerations were based on an overly simple mode l system with only two active
orbitals. Despite this, the examples might serve as an indic ator that high-rank vertices can
be omitted in more realistic systems as well.
6.2 Some numerical results
The quality of our proposed ic-MRCC approximations is teste d on PES calculations. The
PES is calculated by adding the nuclear repulsion term to the electronic energy at each
nuclear conﬁguration R:
EPES (R) = E(R) +
∑
A<B
ZAZB
|RA − RB| ,
electronic energy E(R) = Eref (R) + ∆ E(R) being made up of reference energy Eref = ⟨H⟩
and correction ∆ E calculated with one of the methods in question.
The primary test cases include systems undergoing covalent bond dissociations: the single
and double bond dissociation of H 2O and the triple bond dissociation of N 2. The
BeH2 →Be+H2 reaction pathway provides an example for a process involvin g qualitative
change of molecular structure. Another process involving c hange of structure, the square-
to-rectangle transformation of H 4 is also examined. In the case of these strongly correlated
or structure changing systems, Full Conﬁguration Interact ion (FCI) serves as benchmark,
calculated with an in-house implementation of Olsen’s algo rithm [ 161]. The one exception
is the case of N 2 bond dissociation, where the semistochastic heatbath CI (s hCI) of Sharma
et al. [ 162] is used for this purpose.
The performance of GVB-rCCD(UGA) is also assessed on weakly correlated systems,
involving calculation of conformational barriers for NH 3, H 2O2 and H 2O, deprotonation
energy for H 2O and CH 3OH and dispersion interactions of He and Ne dimers. These res ults
are tested against HF-CCSD(T) energies calculated with the Gaussian 09 software [ 163].
For the sake of comparison, results obtained with another MR correction method, the GVB-
based Extended RPA (GVB-ERPA) of Pernal et al. [ 55, 56] are also presented.
All calculations based on the GVB reference were performed w ith the Budapest version of
the MUNGAUSS program package [ 164]. General details of the calculations were discussed
in Sec. 6.1.
The rCCD(UGA) calculations were presented in Ref. [ 138], except for the dispersion energy
curves which appear in Ref. [ 147]. All other calculations involving idCCSD, idCCSD+ λF
and rmCCD were presented in Ref. [ 137].
6.2.1 Covalent bond dissociations
Single bond dissociation of H 2O
As discussed in Sec.
2.2.1, strongly orthogonal geminal wave functions are ideal choi ces for
a qualitatively correct description of a single dissociati ng covalent bond. The single bond

104.5◦ bond angle and ROH ′ = 1.000 Å bond length for the non-dissociating bond. Total

dissociation of H 2O, depicted in Fig. 6.1 thus illustrates the capabilities of the dressed
interaction vertex schemes in the most ideal situation, fro m the point of view of reliability of
the reference. A bird’s eye view on the PES, shown in the left p anel indicates the success of
correction methods in reducing the ∼100 mEh overall error of GVB. It is also apparent in the
left panel, that the proposed MRCC methods outperform both H F-CCSD and GVB-ERPA
in the dissociated regime (the latter two giving rather simi lar results).
The right and bottom panels serve for comparing the various M R-CC approaches. Diﬀerence
curves taken with FCI indicate that in spite of idCCSD being m ore elaborate than
rCCD(UGA), it brings a deterioration in numerical terms. Th e absolute error of idCCSD
builds up from ∼2 mEh at around equilibrium, to ∼10 mEh upon dissociation. This is to
be compared with the largest error of rCCD(UGA), that is arou nd 4 mE h. The rmCCD
model, which in a sense involves less than idCCSD but more tha n rCCD(UGA), performs in
between. It represents an underestimation of the total ener gy similarly to the observation
made in single determinantal studies [ 70], but its error is smaller and varies on a narrower
114
Some numerical results
range compared with idCCSD. It is important to see that these three methods (idCCSD,
rmCCD, rCCD(UGA)) share a common problem: they all predict a qualitatively incorrect
shape for the dissociation curve displaying an unphysical l ocal maximum around 3 Å (cf.
the right and bottom panels of Fig.
6.1). GVB-ERPA performs better in this respect, as it
does not display such local maxima.
The best performing dressed vertex scheme is the one includi ng not only intravertex, but
intervertex correlation, denoted idCCSD+ λF. Besides reducing the error to the ∼2 mE h
regime along the entire curve, it does not show any local maxi mum.
Among the MR-CC schemes, the performance of idCCSD varies on the broadest scale, as
it provides the smallest error in absolute value at around eq uilibrium distance, but also
the worse estimate in the dissociated regime. We remark that the phenomenon is not
accompanied by idCCSD amplitudes growing unduly large upon bond breaking.
The above results hint that a more carefully designed inclus ion of terms involving cumulant
elements possibly growing large along the PES may represent a cure to the dissociation
problem whilst staying within an MRCCSD framework.
Double bond dissociation of H 2O
The double bond dissociation of H 2O, depicted in Fig.
6.2 shows a behaviour of idCCSD
similar to the previous example. It outperforms others at ar ound equilibrium, providing an
error on the order of 1 mE h until 1.2 Å bond length, but the error grows into the regime
of 50 mE h as the bonds are stretched further. As the bottom panel of Fig . 6.2 reﬂects,
both idCCSD+λF and rmCCD outperform idCCSD in terms of overall error along the bond
breaking process. The error curve of idCCSD+ λF in the bottom panel is of similar quality
as that of rCCD(UGA), starting with a couple of mE h around equilibrium and extending
to ∼15 mEh upon dissociation. Lack of a local maximum is, however, agai n an obvious
advantage of idCCSD+ λF over rCCD(UGA). The rmCCD model is extremely successful
here in keeping the absolute error in the couple of mE h regime along the entire process. A
slight hump, hardly discernible in the right panel is presen t though on the rmCCD curve at
around 3 Å . Hump of rCCD(UGA) is somewhat more pronounced.
The ERPA correction shows a similar behaviour to the case of s ingle bond dissociation: its
error grows quite large, however, no local maximum is observ ed on the dissociation curve.
In order to demonstrate the eﬀect of cumulants involved in dr essed one- and two-body
vertices, a systematic study is carried out on the GVB-rCCD( UGA) description of the H 2O
double dissociation, the results being shown in Fig.
6.3. In this study, the rCCD(UGA)
method is further approximated by neglecting various rank K cumulant contributions in the
dressed one- and two-body vertices. Retained cumulant rank s are shown in parentheses; the
acronym UGA is neglected for brevity.
Neglecting all cumulants of rank two and higher deﬁnes GVB-r CCD(Λ 1), essentially a
single determinantal rCCD with fractional occupation numb ers. This HF-like approximation
apparently inherits the divergent behaviour of restricted single determinantal rCCD [
70], as
no converged GVB-rCCD( Λ 1) solution can be found beyond ∼1.8 Å.
Since cumulants decay fast with increasing rank around equi librium, it is understandable

that inclusion of two-body cumulants already yields quite g ood results around 1 Å . This
scheme, denoted GVB-rCCD( Λ 1, Λ 2) gives a ﬁnite but rather disappointing solution in the
dissociating regime, where higher rank cumulants are in gen eral not negligible. Accordingly,
GVB-rCCD(Λ 1, Λ 2) produces a large hump in the spin recoupling region and tend s to an
energy value rather close to idCCSD, lying oﬀ from FCI by some −50 mEh in the dissociation
limit.
The scheme GVB-rCCD( Λ 1, Λ 2, Λ 3) produces an even more expressed hump and tends
essentially to the same limit as GVB-rCCD( Λ 1, Λ 2). This is not surprising: all components
of Λ 3 belonging to the dissociating geminal vanish, as was discus sed in Secs.
2.1.4 and 2.2.2.
Allowing Λ 4 to enter, but discarding Λ 3 deﬁnes GVB-rCCD( Λ 1, Λ 2, Λ 4) giving acceptable
results. Curiously, GVB-rCCD( Λ 1, Λ 2, Λ 4) stays somewhat more close to FCI than GVB-
rCCD(UGA) with full inclusion of cumulants (denoted GVB-rC CD(Λ 1, Λ 2, Λ 3, Λ 4) in Fig.
6.3).
The above results conﬁrm what one would expect based on Fig. 2.1 of Sec. 2.2.2,
and one can generally conclude that retaining Λ 4 in the dressed two-body vertex is
116
Some numerical results
essential to obtain correct PES for covalent bond dissociat ion. We remark that the
success of GVB-rCCD( Λ 1, Λ 2, Λ 4) originates from a fortuitous rather than systematic error
compensation taking place in this example. Scheme GVB-rCCD (Λ 1, Λ 2, Λ 4) outperforming
GVB-rCCD(UGA) does not occur in general.

Symmetric dissociation of the H 2O molecule serves for one further test, once again
demonstrated on rCCD(UGA): examination of the numerical eﬀ ect of various excitation
types as well as the role of excitations aﬀected by redundanc y. Results are presented at two

excitations retained in a given scheme ( nexc), collected in Table 6.1, has to be contested with
10107, the number of original excitations when following th e pruning described in Sec. 6.1.1.
Note that individual excitations or blocks of excitations w ith all overlap eigenvalues below
the numerical threshold are already excluded in the origina l scheme. The total number of
excitations as well as the structure of the redundant blocks remain unchanged when stepping
from ROH = 1.0 Å to ROH = 4.0 Å.

Bond dissociation of N 2
The example of N 2 dissociation (depicted in Fig.

results in the dissociated region, in parallel with the GVB r eference becoming gradually
less reliable in this sequence of test cases. The idCCSD meth od is acceptable at around
equilibrium, but the dissociation curve shape is deﬁcient, exhibiting a hump at around
2 Å , reminiscent of single determinantal CC methods. Errors taken with shCI, shown
in the bottom panel of Fig. 6.4 allow to draw a conclusion similar to the H 2O double
dissociation case, with the extent of discrepancies increa sed. The error of both rCCD(UGA)
and idCCSD+ λF amount to 50-70 mE h in the dissociated regime. At variance with
previous observations, rCCD(UGA) deviates signiﬁcantly f rom other CC methods at around
equilibrium. The rmCCD model is again the most successful me thod regarding overall error.
While the negative shift of rmCCD with respect to other CC app roximations persists, its
error remains in the positive domain. Albeit the error of rmC CD is the smallest among the
MR-CC methods tested, it is markedly larger when compared wi th the previous example

6.2.2 Structural changes
The BeH 2 system
The BeH2 →Be+H2 reaction pathway, as computed by Purvis and Bartlett [
165], has become
a widely used test of performance in the presence of strong co rrelation. A further numerical
study for our correction methods is carried out on this class ical MR test case, using the same
double-ζ quality basis set and the same geometry points proposed by th e aforementioned
authors. In particular, beryllium is placed in the origin of the coordinate system and the
119
Some numerical results
position of the two hydrogen atoms is given by (dBe-H2, ±dH), withdH = 1.344 Å −0.46dBe-H2.
Values for dBe-H2 in Å (with corresponding points of Purvis et al. indicated in parenthesis)
are as follows: 0.000 (A), 0.529 (B), 1.058 (C), 1.322 (D), 1. 455 (E), 1.588 (F), 1.852 (G),
2.117 (H), 3.175 (I).
The diﬃculty of the BeH2 example lies with a change in the Lewis structure along the re action
pathway: in geometry points A-E (till about dBe-H2 = 1.5 Å ) the two valence geminals
can be assigned to the two Be-H bonds; however, starting at po int F, geminals of the
the lowest energy GVB solution can rather be identiﬁed as one residing on atom Be and
another describing the H-H bond. The change in the nature of t he reference is necessarily
accompanied by a discontinuity on potential curves.
A pair of single excitations with imaginary excitation ener gy are excluded from the ERPA
correction at geometry points E and I. The rCCD(UGA) calcula tion is also tweaked at point
E: excitated states :Err
pq : |Φ GVB ⟩ and :Ess
pq: |Φ GVB ⟩ (orbitalsφp,φr andφq,φs constituting the
two BeH geminals, respectively) had to be omitted from T in order to achieve convergence.
No such tinkering was required for the convergence of the oth er three CC approaches.
Fig.
6.5 shows the results. Comparison with the FCI benchmark shows t hat GVB-ERPA
describes a larger portion of the correlation than GVB-rCCD (UGA) in this example. Fig.
6.5. also reﬂects that parallelity of GVB-rCCD is slightly bett er: diﬀerence from FCI lies in
the range 3-29 mE h for GVB-ERPA (peaking at point E), and 13-30 mE h for GVB-rCCD.
On the other hand, idCCSD, idCCSD+ λF and rmCCD all perform much better on this
system, their error being in the range of a few mE h-s at most (also see Table 6.2).

CASCCSD(sw) is a single determinantal-like formulation du e to Lyakh and coworkers [
166],
adapted for the MR situation following the basic idea of Ivan ov and Adamowicz [ 134];
abbreviation "sw" in the acronym refers to a swap of the large st weight determinant around
dBe-H2 = 1 .322 Å. The Mk-MRCCSD of Mukherjee et al. [ 112] and the ic-MRCCSD of
Evangelista and Gauss [ 117] both fall in the category of genuine Hilbert-space MRCC
techniques, harnessing a JM-type and an internally contrac ted parametrization, respectively.
As an ic-MRCC model, the latter bears the most kinship with ou r approach. The reference
underlying these three methods is a CAS(2,2) function (CAS s tanding for Complete Active
Space).
The energy diﬀerences of Table
6.2 reﬂect that the performance of GVB-rCCD(UGA) lags
behind MR-based CCSD schemes by roughly two orders of magnit ude. This holds true
regarding either of the benchmark methods. The other GVB-ba sed methods fare better:
in particular, idCCSD proves to be the best dressed vertex-b ased method for this system.
The fact that rmCCD and idCCSD+ λF are somewhat less successful in this example, points
to the need of careful investigation of beyond-idCCSD terms in various strong correlation
situations. Swap in the GVB description of the system occurr ing between 1.455 and 1.588
Å for dBe-H2 is accompanied by a slight increase in the error of idCCSD. No tably, idCCSD
errors are in the range of those by ic-MRCCSD up to dBe-H2 = 1.5 Å. Errors of internally
contracted methods fall behind CASCCSD(sw), compelling fu rther improvement of ic-MRCC
approaches.

alleviated much by the correction methods, the error of GVB i s reduced considerably by

6.2.3 Conformational barriers
The GVB model accounts for the interaction of chemical bonds only at the SCF level. The
interest in torsional barriers described by geminal based m ethods [
56, 167] derives from this
fact, since covalent bonds are essentially conserved but th eir interaction changes during such
processes. The three examples picked here are the bending mo tion of H 2O and the umbrella
inversion of NH 3 in the left and right panel of Fig. 6.7, as well as the torsional motion of
H2O2 in the bottom panel of Fig. 6.7.
Fig. 6.7 uniformly shows a considerable improvement over GVB both by rCCD(UGA) and
ERPA. The rCCD(UGA) correction is closer to the CCSD(T) than ERPA for H 2O bending
and H 2O2 torsion. The quality of rCCD(UGA) and ERPA are also comparab le when tested
against CCSD(T) on the example of NH 3 inversion, the error curves in the bottom right panel
of Fig. 6.7 falling in the kcal mol −1 range. According to the top right panel, rCCD(UGA)
barriers are slightly better in this example.

the CCSD(T) benchmark we see that the error of the dissociati on energy is on the order
of 10 kcal mol −1 by GVB. This is reduced to a couple of kcal mol −1 both by GVB-ERPA

6.2.5 Dispersion energies
Here dispersion energy calculations are presented for dime rs He 2 and Ne 2. Both calculations
were carried out using the Dunning aug-cc-pVDZ atomic basis set [
168] (with ﬁve d-orbitals).
In reporting the results, Edisp stands for the total energy of the supersystem minus total
energies of the subsystems, computed according to the Boys- Bernardi scheme [ 169].
Equilibrium atomic distances and the corresponding disper sion energies can be seen in Table
6.4, while Fig. 6.8 shows the dispersion energy curves for He 2 and Ne2, respectively. Just like
HF, GVB cannot describe dispersion interactions, resultin g in non-bonding energy curves.
GVB-ERPA and GVB-rCCD(UGA) improve signiﬁcantly on this si tuation, although the
results are far from perfect. While GVB-ERPA underestimate s the dispersion energy in both
cases, GVB-rCCD(UGA) overestimates it, although to a somew hat lesser extent. A more
signiﬁcant diﬀerence shows up in the equilibrium atomic dis tances: GVB-ERPA overshoots
by 0.4 bohr in each case, while GVB-rCCD(UGA) reproduces the benchmark CCSD(T)
value to the digits displayed in Table 6.4.
For the sake of fairness, we remark that deﬁciencies of GVB-E RPA have been noted before
in describing weak interaction, and electron group embeddi ng has been suggested as a cure
(GVB-EERPA) [ 57, 58]. It has also been demonstrated that GVB-EERPA represents a
signiﬁcant improvement over GVB-ERPA in such situations.
The algebraic ring approximation rCCD(UGA) as applied to a H F reference is also presented
in Fig.
6.8 and Table 6.4. The results of HF-rCCD(UGA) and GVB-rCCD(UGA) are very
similar. This is not surprising: the electron correlation i s so weak, that the problem is
essentially of single determinantal nature, and the dresse d interaction vertices of GVB-
rCCD(UGA) barely diﬀer from their bare counterparts. Howev er, the small inﬂuence of
cumulant dressing still changes the results in the right dir ection, as both equilibrium energies
and distances are improved in GVB-rCCD(UGA) compared to HF- rCCD(UGA).

Chapter 7
Conclusion and outlook
In the previous chapters, a novel formulation of the ic-MRCC theory has been worked out,
based on the notion of cumulant-dependent dressed interact ion vertices. Many appealing
features of the formalism have been demonstrated, such as a s traightforward diagrammatic
construction of equations, clear interpretation based on a n expansion formally resembling
MBPT, size extensivity and consistency over singlet subsys tems and invariance under unitary
transformations in the subspace of core or virtual orbitals .
Four simple approximations of the general theory have been p roposed based on diagrammatic
(idCCSD, rmCCD, idCCSD+λF), or algebraic (rCCD(UGA)) arguments.
Performance of these methods has been assessed in Chapter
6 on strongly and weakly
correlated molecular systems using a GVB reference. The obt ained numerical results indicate
that treating only the intravertex part of electron correla tion is in general not suﬃcient to
properly describe strongly correlated systems. The idCCSD approximation (which may
have appeared to be quite a natural approximation) turned ou t to perform poorly for
bond dissociation processes, both in quantitative and qual itative terms. While rmCCD and
rCCD(UGA) typically produced results much closer to benchm ark values, both methods
lead to qualitatively wrong dissociation curves displayin g unphysical local maxima. The
only approach that does not showcase such qualitative error s is idCCSD+λF incorporating
some portion of intervertex correlation.
The above numerical results naturally raise the question of how to ﬁnd manageable yet
accurate approximations in the general ic-MRCC theory. Est imating the magnitude of
individual diagrams (or diagram classes) poses the main diﬃ culty in this respect. The results
clearly suggest that intuition gained from the diagrammati c analysis of single determinantal
theories may not lead to the correct conclusions in the stron gly correlated regime.
Furthermore, one cannot neglect the possibility of compens ation eﬀects between diagrams:
it is by no means obvious whether blindly adding further expl icitly cumulant-dependent
diagrams to our equations would improve the results.
Dependence on the reference is manifest in the appearance of cumulants in diagrams. In
order to assess the importance of certain diagrams, one shou ld analyse which elements of
cumulants grow large in the presence of strong correlation, and how these large elements are
connected to interaction vertices in diﬀerent terms.
127
Such an analysis should be carried out in the future for GVB to ﬁnd out which diagrammatic
contributions should be further included (or conversely, w hich contributions should be
omitted) to arrive at a well-balanced ic-MRCC method. Given the conceptual advantages
of the outlined theory, it would deﬁnitely be worth taking th is extra eﬀort.
On a more speculative note, let us brieﬂy mention two further possibilities oﬀered by this
general framework.
As another route to ﬁnd simple ic-MRCC models, one may consid er drastically reducing the
number of excited states in the parametrization of T . We deﬁne orbitals φi with ni ≥ 1/2
and orbitals φa with na < 1/2 (that is, ¯na > 1/2) to represent hole and particle states,
respectively. This is a stricter categorization than the on e given in Sec.
2.3.4, as it makes
the sets of hole and particle states disjoint. Only excitati ons from hole states to particle
states are allowed; while this would lead to the loss of vario us excited states of |Φ ⟩ (such
as spectators and deexcitations; cf. Sec. 6.2.1), the computational advantages would be
prominent. With the disjoint hole-particle sets, one does n ot need to keep diﬀerent arrays
for the diﬀerent variants of dressed vertices (correspondi ng to the diﬀerent leg orientations):
one could store e.g. all elements of F and ˜V in single arrays of size M 2 andM 4, respectively.
Also, this categorization would naturally rule out the prob lematic excitations of Sec. 6.1.1,
as well as the redundancy of the excitation manifold (the lat ter being a consequence of having
formally the same excitations as in a single determinantal t heory).
From a more conceptual point of view, this separation would a llow us to deﬁne a dressed
MRGNO Hamiltonian in terms of dressed interaction vertices :
¯H =

Of course, the same diagrams are contained in these equation s as in Sec.

assuming a GVB reference, the strict hole-particle separat ion combined with the sparse
structure of GVB cumulants makes many classes of diagrams vanish (e.g. the second, seventh,
twelfth, and thirteenth diagrams of Eq. ( 3.3.7)), leading to a more transparent theory. In
this simpliﬁed framework, one could possibly have a clearer view on which diagrams are
important in the presence of strong correlation.
Another interesting possibility is the calculation of vari ous molecular properties with the help
of ic-MRCC density matrices. Starting from the deﬁnition of Eq. (
2.1.12) and essentially
following the single determinantal treatment of Ref. [ 26], one could ﬁnd
¯γK = ⟨ :exp(T †):aK :exp(T ):⟩
⟨ :exp(T †)::exp(T ):⟩ = ⟨ :exp(T †):aK :exp(T ):⟩L
128
for the K-RDM calculated with the ic-MRCC wave function. Subscript L denotes the linked
expectation value, as in Sec. 3.1. Applying the MRGNO and the cumulant expansion of
density matrices to this formula helps us to identify ic-MRC C density cumulants as
¯λK =λK + ⟨ :exp(T †)::aK::exp(T ):⟩C ,
where λK is just the cumulant calculated with our starting MR functio n. This formally
exact expression gives ic-MRCC cumulants as an expansion of connected terms. However,
simplicity of the above formula is deceptive, as evaluation of the right hand side with the
MRGWT becomes extremely complicated even for small powers o f T , T †. Diagrammatic
techniques (similar to the single determinantal treatment of Ref. [ 170]) might facilitate this
process as well. Finding reasonable (e.g. N-representable) approximations for ¯λK could
provide us with ways to accurately calculate various properties of strongly correlated systems.
Addressing these questions remains to be a project for the fu ture.

The relationship between ﬁrst and
second quantization
First and second quantization are equivalent formulations of the same theory, and in this

Appendix B
The role of cumulants − a minimal
example
In order to discuss the importance and interpretation of cum ulants, it is instructive to look
at a simple model system with a well-known exact solution; fo r this reason, let us consider
the minimal LCAO description of a dissociated H 2 molecule. The discussion is mostly based
on Ref. [
171].
We have a bonding ("gerade") and an unbonding ("ungerade") s patial orbital:
φg(r) = 1√
2 [χA(r) +χB(r)] , φu(r) = 1√
2 [χA(r) −χB(r)] ,
χA(r) and χB(r) being hydrogenic 1s functions localized on inﬁnitely separated nuclei.
Because of this inﬁnite separation, terms like χA(r)χB(r) always vanish.
Two determinants contribute to the exact wave function: the usual HF determinant
Φ g¯g(r1σ1, r2σ2) = φg(r1)φg(r2)Θ( σ1,σ 2) ,
and the doubly excited determinant
Φ u¯u(r1σ1, r2σ2) = φu(r1)φu(r2)Θ( σ1,σ 2) .
Here Θ( σ1,σ 2) is a singlet spin function:
Θ( σ1,σ 2) = 1√
2 [α(σ1)β(σ2) −α(σ2)β(σ1)] .
The exact wave function can be written as
Ψ( r1σ1, r2σ2) = 1√
2 [Φ g¯g(r1σ1, r2σ2) − Φ u¯u(r1σ1, r2σ2)]
= 1√
2 [φg(r1)φg(r2) −φu(r1)φu(r2)]Θ( σ1,σ 2)
= 1√
2 [χA(r1)χB(r2) +χB(r1)χA(r2)]Θ( σ1,σ 2) .
135
The last equality shows that Ψ describes a purely covalent system with exactly one electro n
localized around each nuclei. Using Ψ , it is easy to calculate the spin-free density matrices
as (cf. Eq. ( A.0.6)). For the 1-RDM we ﬁnd
Γ( r1; r′
1) = χA(r1)χA(r′
1) +χB(r1)χB(r′
1) .
One then immediately has the electron density ρ(r1) = Γ( r1; r1) too:
ρ(r1) = χ2
A(r1) +χ2
B(r1) .
It is also easy to ﬁnd the 2-RDM. For the sake of brevity, we onl y quote its diagonal part,
the pair density:
ρ(r1, r2) = χ2
A(r1)χ2
B(r2) +χ2
B(r1)χ2
A(r2) = ρcov(r1, r2)
Again, the pair density formula mirrors the localized and co valent nature of our system: the
only event with a non-negligible probability is ﬁnding elec tron 1 around nucleus A while
electron 2 is around nucleus B (or the other way around).
To highlight the importance of cumulants, we decompose the pair density as (cf. Eq. (
2.1.30))
ρ(r1, r2) = Λ( r1, r2) +ρ(r1)ρ(r2) − 1
2 |Γ( r1; r2)|2
The ﬁrst term of the expansion is the cumulant part; the secon d and third terms constitute
the wedge product part. The wedge product part simply reads
ρ(r1)ρ(r2) − 1
2 |Γ( r1; r2)|2 = 1
2[χ2
A(r1)χ2
A(r2) +χ2
B(r1)χ2
B(r2)]
+χ2
A(r1)χ2
B(r2) +χ2
B(r1)χ2
A(r2)
= 1
2ρion(r1, r2) +ρcov(r1, r2) ,
hence the cumulant part must be
Λ( r1, r2) = −1
2ρion(r1, r2) .
This is a negative contribution to the pair density with a sim ple interpretation. Let us
compare our results with the analogous expressions in HF app roximation (i.e. Ψ ≈ Φ g¯g):
Γ HF (r1; r′
1) =χA(r1)χA(r′
1) +χA(r1)χB(r′
1)
+χB(r1)χA(r′
1) +χB(r1)χB(r′
1) ,
ρHF (r1) = χ2
A(r1) +χ2
B(r1) = ρ(r1) ,
and
ρHF (r1, r2) = ρHF (r1)ρHF (r2) − 1
2 |Γ HF (r1; r2)|2
= 1
2ρHF (r1)ρHF (r2)
= 1
2 [ρion(r1, r2) +ρcov(r1, r2)]
136
The pair density in HF approximation is a mixture of ionic and covalent contributions. This
is just the manifestation of the well-known deﬁciency of a si ngle determinantal description,
in which electrons with diﬀerent spins are uncorrelated, an d therefore can be easily localized
on the same atomic site. The wedge product terms of the exact p air density have a very
similar structure: although the covalent contribution app ears with a larger weight, there are
still remaining ionic terms. Calculating the pair density s imply as a wedge product would
then lead to a fundamentally ﬂawed description of the system . The role of the cumulant
term is to cancel the erroneous ionic terms and decrease the p robability of ﬁnding electrons
near each other, thus forming the correct covalent conﬁgura tion. In this sense, cumulants
are responsible for the left-right electron correlation.
Admittedly, the above simple example is somewhat artiﬁcial : when knowing the exact pair
density, there is no reason to try a wedge product approximat ion. Still, the model showcases
the importance of cumulants in forming the correct probabil istic description of strongly
correlated systems by cancelling spurious contributions c oming from the factorizable part of
the density.

Appendix C
Spin-summation of density matrices
and cumulants
Evaluating the spin-summed form of a product of spin orbital quantities (like density matrices
or cumulants) is highly non-trivial. However, as it was poin ted out ﬁrst by Shamasundar [
38],
the summation can − at least in principle − always be performed by ﬁrst considering the
"inverse problem", that is, expressing the spin orbital qua ntities in terms of spin-free ones.
The validity of these inversion formulas can be proven by stu dying permutation groups
[172]. However, in this appendix we present a more straightforwa rd derivation for them,
generalizing the concepts of Ref [ 22]. We shall consider singlet states, although the results
remain valid for spin-averaged ensembles of high-multipli city states as well. Latin indices
now denote spatial orbitals.
We discuss spin-summation of density matrices throughout t his appendix. However, in the
end, we will generalize the results to other quantities (cum ulants, interaction vertices, CC
amplitudes) as well.
Spin symmetry imposes serious constraints on elements of de nsity matrices. An obvious
constraint is posed by MS conservation: elements such as γpα
qβ or γpαrα
qβsα (having diﬀerent
number of α spins in the upper and lower indices, thus not conserving MS) must vanish by
virtue of spin orthogonality.
Let us now consider a simple two dimensional rotation, that i s, an element ofSO(2) ⊂SU (2):

We have derived the above results for density matrices. Howe ver, we have never really used
any speciﬁc property of density matrices; we only requiered MS-conservation, antisymmetry
andSU (2) invariance. This means that all the above formulae stand for any antisymmetric

conditions by construction, as well as Coupled Cluster ampl itudes and matrix elements of
second quantized operators (particularly, the Fockian and the antisymmetrized two-electron
integral in the Hamiltonian). Furthermore, the results rem ain naturally valid for tensors
obtained by contraction of two or more tensors: the most impo rtant examples are the dressed
K-particle interaction vertices introduced in Secs.

Let us ﬁnally comment on the non-locality of dressed vertice s. A vertex is called local, if it
can only aﬀect the propagation of each particle or hole in a si ngle point of space, respectively.
In this sense, the bare electron-electron interaction is lo cal:
˜v(x1,x 2;x′
1,x ′
2) = ˆv(x1,x 2)[δ(x1,x ′
1)δ(x2,x ′
2) −δ(x1,x ′
2)δ(x2,x ′
1)] ,
where
ˆv(x1,x 2) = 1
|r1 − r2| .
This can be seen by using Eq. ( 2.1.8) and the completeness of {φp(x)} in Eq. ( D.0.5). On
the other hand, the folded electron-electron interaction g ives rise to a non-local exchange
term in the Fockian:
f (x;x′) =
[
−1
2 ∇2 −
∑
A
ZA
|r − RA| +
∫
dyˆv(x,y )ρ(y)
]
δ(x,x ′) − ˆv(x,x ′)γ(x;x′) .
Hence, during the real space propagation of particles and ho les, the Fockian can connect
distant points x and x′.
Besides containing these Fockian parts, a further layer of n on-locality is added to dressed
vertices in the form of cumulant-involving internal proces ses. Repeated scattering on
cumulants (all possible points taken into account by integr ation) can make dressed vertices
aﬀect incoming and outgoing particles or holes in diﬀerent p oints of space. This is not
surprising: non-locality arises whenever many-body eﬀect s are folded into eﬀective fewer-
body quantities (a possible example given by CC amplitudes) .
While strictly speaking, dressed vertices are non-local, t heir range is limited to certain parts
of space if orbitals and cumulants are localized. For exampl e, in the case of strongly
orthogonal geminal wave functions like APSG, cumulants are centered around localized
electron pairs (e.g. covalent bonds) and decay fast for larg e distances (see Sec. 2.2.2).
In such a case, due to connectedness, non-local dressing eﬀe cts are dominant only along the
spatial extent of bonds.

Abstract
Perturbative or Coupled Cluster-type correction methods built on a single Slater-determinant
tend to perform poorly in the presence of strong electron cor relation (a common example
being molecules with dissociating covalent bonds), raisin g the need for an MR treatment
employing a linear combination of determinants as referenc e function. This dissertation
focuses on analytical and numerical studies concerning the so-called internally contracted
Multi-Reference Coupled Cluster (ic-MRCC) model, a genera l theory aiming to accurately
describe strongly correlated many-electron systems.
The starting point of the investigation is the normal ordere d ic-MRCC parametrization of
Mukherjee et al. Derivations are carried out with the help of the MR generalization of
operator normal ordering and the corresponding Wick theore m, as developed by Kutzelnigg
and Mukherjee. The novelty of the proposed approach lies in t he introduction of the so-
called dressed interaction vertices. Dressed one- and two- body interaction vertices formally
resemble the Fockian and the electron-electron interactio n vertices of a single determinantal
theory, and one can use them in an analogous way to derive the w orking equations of the
formalism. However, dressed vertices are functions of so-c alled density cumulants (which
carry the correlation content of the reference), and for thi s reason, they are more suitable for
strongly correlated scenarios than their "bare" counterpa rts. Working with dressed vertices
makes the otherwise complicated derivations of the ic-MRCC equations considerably easier.
Construction and plausible interpretation of dressed vert ices are facilitated by diagrammatic
techniques.
In this thesis, a completely general ic-MRCC theory based on the concept of dressed vertices
is developed. Algebraic and diagrammatic techniques are wo rked out in detail for the
construction of these vertices, and similar techniques are used when applying them in the
derivation of ic-MRCC formulae. We present a diagrammatic a nalysis of CC amplitudes and
the energy correction formula in order to highlight the role of dressed vertices in electron
correlation. We also discuss diagrammatic approaches for t he singlet spin-adaptation of the
theory.
The manifold of elementary excitations used in ic-MRCC is ty pically linearly dependent, and
one must treat this redundancy in order to avoid possible num erical instabilities. A novel
approach is discussed on the treatment of this problem.
As an interesting sidenote, we investigate typically weak- correlation phenomena, dispersion
interactions in the ic-MRCC framework. We demonstrate the e mergence of the London
dispersion formula for an approximation of the general theo ry.
Based on diagrammatic and algebraic arguments, we propose s imple approximations to the
general ic-MRCC theory. These include a model formally rese mbling traditional CCSD (with
bare vertices replaced by dressed ones), ring-like approxi mations to the former, as well as a
more involved scheme incorporating some of the so-called in tervertex electron correlation.
Applying these approximate correction schemes to a simple g eminal wave function, we test
the quality of the proposed models on diﬀerent, strongly or w eakly correlated molecular
systems. The examples involve the breaking of covalent bond s and structural rearrangements,
as well as the calculation of conformational barriers and di spersion interactions. We discuss
both strengths and weaknesses of the proposed approximatio ns.

Összefoglaló
Az ún. erős elektronkorreláció (pl. disszociáló kovalens k ötéseket tartalmazó molekulák
esetén) jelentősen rontja a Slater-determinánsra épülő pe rturbatív vagy Coupled Cluster
korrekciók megbízhatóságát. Ilyen esetekben több determi náns lineáris kombinációjaként
előálló Multi-Referencia (MR) hullámfüggvények alkalmaz ása szükséges. Jelen disszertáció
az ún. "internally contracted Multi-Reference Coupled Clu ster" (ic-MRCC) elmélet
képletszintű és numerikus vizsgálatát mutatja be.
A modell kiindulópontját a Mukherjee és munkatársai által j avasolt normálrendezett ic-
MRCC parametrizáció adja. A levezetéseket a Kutzelnigg és M ukherjee által bevezetett,
MR-hullámfüggvényekre általánosított normálrendezés és Wick-tétel segítségével végezzük.
A megközelítésünk újdonsága az ún. "öltöztetett" (dressed ) kölcsönhatási vertexek
bevezetésében áll. A dressed egy- és kétrészecske vertexek formálisan a Fock- és az elektron-
elektron kölcsönhatási vertexekre emlékeztetnek, és ezek kel analóg szerepet játszanak a
CC egyenletek levezetésében. Az elektronkorrelációt kódo ló ún. sűrűségkumulánsoktól
való függésük révén azonban alkalmasabbak erősen korrelál t rendszerek leírására, mint
"csupasz" megfelelőik. A dressed vertexek jelentősen megk önnyítik az ic-MRCC
egyenletek (máskülönben kezelhetetlenül bonyolulttá vál ó) levezetését; maguk a dressed
vertexek egyszerűen megszerkeszthetők és interpretálhat ók Feynman-diagramos módszerek
segítségével.
A disszertációban egy általános ic-MRCC elmélet kiépítésé t mutatjuk be dressed vertexek
segítségével. Algebrai és diagramos módszerekkel is tárgy aljuk ezen vertexek, majd
a segítségükkel felépülő ic-MRCC egyenletek levezetését. Diagram alapú módszerekkel
vizsgáljuk a dressed vertexek hatását a CC amplitudókra, va lamint a számolt
energiakorrekcióra. Hasonló módon mutatjuk be az elmélet s zinglet rendszerekre történő
spinadaptálását is.
Az ic-MRCC számításokhoz felhasznált elemi gerjesztések l ineárisan összefüggő rendszert
alkotnak: ezen redundanciát kezelni kell, az esetleges num erikus problémákat elkerülendő.
Ennek kapcsán egy új redundanciakezelő eljárás is bemutatá sra kerül.
Egy érdekes mellékágként a London-féle diszperziós kölcsö nhatások leírását is vizsgáljuk az
ic-MRCC formalizmus egy közelítésének keretében.
Diagramos és algebrai érvekre támaszkodva, egyszerű közel ítéseket javaslunk az általános
ic-MRCC elméletre: egy formálisan a hagyományos CCSD-re em lékeztető modellt (dressed
vertexekkel helyettesítve az eredeti, csupasz vertexeket ), ún. ring-diagram alapú közelítő
modelleket, valamint egy bonyolultabb módszert, ami az ele ktronkorreláció ún. intervertex
részét is ﬁgyelembe tudja venni.
Egy egyszerű geminál hullámfüggvényre alkalmazva ezeket a módszereket, különböző
erősen, vagy gyengén korrelált rendszereken teszteljük a h atékonyságukat. A tesztek során
disszociáló kovalens kötések, szerkezetátrendeződések, konformációs energiagátak, valamint
diszperziós kölcsönhatások leírását vizsgáljuk. A módsze rek erősségeit és gyengeségeit
egyformán diszkutáljuk.
